## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 274 — Tuesday the fifteenth of November 2016, from about ten o'clock at night, in this city

**SEVEN HUNDRED AND TWENTY DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, A WRONG ANSWER ABOUT THAT REGISTER IS BEING REPEATED BY PEOPLE WHO HAVE NEVER MET THE MAN WHO STARTED IT, AND A BETTER WRONG ANSWER IS NOW GOING ROUND WITH IT, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN DID TWO SMALL THINGS IN FRONT OF ELEVEN PEOPLE AND WAS GIVEN NOTHING, A BOILER THAT NOBODY OWNS CAME OUT OF A CUPBOARD UNDER A STAIR AND WENT DOWN A BACK LANE ON A TROLLEY, A FIGURE ON A WATER BILL WENT UP FOR A TAKEOVER FOUR COUNTIES AWAY, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS SEVEN HUNDRED AND TWENTY DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** **Nell Ardent opened it on Tuesday the fifteenth to copy a line out for a form, and opened it again because she does that, and the nineteenth is still a yard light with a bulb in a maintenance column and the bulb is still out, and the line goes down the page and stops about two inches short of the bottom, and she worked it out on the corner of a form with the end of her pen.** She put it back in the cupboard it came out of. The register did not move and there is no name in the margin and there has not been one since about November two years ago.
- **THE WRONG ANSWER ABOUT THE REGISTER IS STILL GOING ROUND AND IS STILL WRONG, AND THERE IS NOW A BETTER WRONG ANSWER GOING ROUND WITH IT.** **On Thursday the thirteenth, in a first-floor room over a shop where there are no chairs and a man leaves the door open, the young woman of about twenty — the only person in that room who was in a room in April — was asked which of the dated things will be the first one somebody can prove, and answered that it will not be a paper at all: it will be the day, the twenty-eighth of June.** Her reason is that a date is the only thing this borough has ever agreed on, one man in a street said it, five people turned up, none of them had been asked, and a date cannot be lost where a docket can. **It was taken in about eleven seconds by a room that had never heard the wrong answer either, the woman of about fifty-five said out loud that it is a great deal better than the register, a woman who works in a shop and a man in his forties and a man off a late and a man who is in none of it all preferred it, and the woman with the two copies in a bag under a chair heard a room agree with something quickly and said nothing.** On the fifteenth both answers are going round the same hall and a woman gives the day across a table the way you give somebody something better than what they had, and neither of them says where it came from, and nobody at that table joins in, and the man in his sixties is four feet away with a cup of tea and does not look up. **THE MAN WHO INVENTED THE FIRST ONE IS NOT IN THAT ROOM, DID NOT ARRANGE IT, IS NOT TOLD AND MUST NOT BE TOLD, AND THE CHAPTER SAYS OUT LOUD THAT HE TOLD A SHOPKEEPER ON THE THIRTIETH OF JULY THAT HE COULD NOT GET THE TWO HALVES OF HIS OWN ANSWER APART, THAT HE WAS STANDING IN A STREET WHEN HE SAID IT, AND THAT NOT ONE OF THAT ROOM WAS IN THAT STREET, AND THAT HE WILL NEVER BE TOLD, AND THAT IS THE CORRECT OUTCOME.**
- **THE QUESTION IS STILL A QUESTION.** On the fifteenth, in the same hall, the man from the parade — who has put his hand up in that room twice since the summer and who was answered on the seventeenth of September by a lad of about eighteen who told the room the whole position of it — did not put his hand up, and nobody noticed that either, and he sat there until ten with his cup in both hands.
- **A SECOND DAY WITH NOTHING WRONG IN IT, AND IT WAS SMALLER AND IT WAS BETTER.** **On the evening of the seventeenth of September the woman of about fifty-five, who had a room over a shop until Monday and nowhere else to be, and the young woman of about twenty, off the college bus, went out separately without having told each other, met at the top of a parade by accident, and walked down it and back and told nobody.** The reason, in her mouth, is that if anybody is told then it is a thing, and then somebody does it twice, and two times is a habit, and then somebody asks how many of them there were, and there is not a number. The woman of about fifty-five said she thought the reason was that nobody asked them to come, and she was told it is the same reason, in one sentence. **The first of those days was five people in the rain for an hour and a half who mostly did not know who else was coming. This one was two of them and a bus. Nothing is built, nobody was hurt, and the boy was on the dishes the whole evening, was not running anything, and was not told.**
- **A CUT-BACK STONE STEP AND A WORD NOBODY CAN EXPLAIN.** On the twenty-fourth of September two men took up the front course of a cracked slab on the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, because a man had said in a doorway in September that it was a trip, and he was not there and was not asked to be. **They disagreed only about the job — take it all up and relay it, which needs a number and a book, or cut it back and re-bed the top course, which has no number on it — and neither of them would put anything in writing, and one of them would not discuss it in a shop.** The man in his sixty asked for one thing to be said out loud in a room somebody hears, which was that he was not asking the other man to do it, and the other man let him say it. **The only thing that survived the morning is a word: the front corner of the new course has gone soft at the arris, and the man in his sixties cannot put that word to anything in the whole of his own trade, and says he has no idea and that the other man said it, and the other man does not explain it and is not asked to.** The slab is standing against the wall by the door under a coat and goes up on the Wednesday, because the doors open on a Wednesday meeting and there is nowhere else in that hall to put it.
- **A TIN OF PAINT WITH A NAME ON THE LID.** A part-used tin in the top drawer of a kitchen unit in a flat on a street off Corrance Lane, with a surname and an initial chinagraphed on masking tape folded under the rim, came back on the Friday of the thirtieth of September with a thumb's worth gone out of it and a dent in the paint, and the gatepost at the end of the yard behind that yard has been greyed over where it has been bare since April. **The man who did odd jobs read a name because of the initial at the front of it, did not know what the tin was, used a bit because there was nothing else to hand, put the lid back on, and said so.** The surname on the tape is never read aloud in that chapter and nobody goes looking for anybody. **Three names are in three places and are never put in one sentence or one list: the surname on the tape in a kitchen, a man's name in the back of a stock book in the lock-up behind the counter of the shop that shuts early, and a list of eleven names with dates and places and sums on it in a book at the back of a room at the end of a corridor on the first floor of a building by the water, which nobody has had out since the summer.**
- **AN ORDINARY WEEK IN OCTOBER, AND A BOROUGH THAT IS NOT COUNTING.** Four men off a firm on a parade went out on Monday the third of October to a house in Corrance Lane and came back on the Thursday, and their hours went on a sheet into a folder in a van and out of that folder on the other side of that water, and nobody in this borough has ever seen that sheet. **Their foreman is a man who is not on anything and stood in a front room waiting for a man to come and sign a sheet and said nothing about the week and was asked nothing about it.** A van came back on the Wednesday with no near mirror on it. A firm of heating engineers put twenty pounds on the figure for coming out. The hall off the parade is opened on a Friday afternoon now. **And at about ten past four on the seventh, shutting the gas off at the meter by the door, the woman who has had the key to that hall for about nine years said that it is the first week she can remember being bored in. Nobody disagreed. She knows that is not much of a thing to have been.**
- **A WATER BILL THAT WENT UP FOR A TAKEOVER FOUR COUNTIES AWAY.** On the twentieth of October, in the second house of Marsh Lane, with a man in her bathroom measuring for a bath screen, **she picked her water bill off the top of the boiler and saw that a standing charge and a unit rate had both gone up, and the cause was four counties over the water on one letterhead since about June, and nothing done in this borough caused it, and she said: *That's nowt to do with us and it's still got my name on it*.** One man heard her say it. **She has been right once, about a thing in June, and said out loud that she would rather be wrong this time, and that she wants to be a woman who is wrong about a number on a piece of paper.** She asked whether she should ring the number at the foot of the bill and was asked who she would ring, and said she did not know. **The bill did not go into the folder with the others.** The trade rate out of the Penhale yard has not moved since about 1987 and is not going to, and that is a different sheet of paper with a different name at the top of it, and she was told so and believed it. **The drain is touched once, in one sentence, by her: a man up the street who did the drains has about the legs he had in February and is not getting them back, and back would be four, and there was nobody to ring about that either.**
- **TWO PEOPLE IN ONE SHOP ON ONE AFTERNOON IN AUGUST, AND A QUEUE SIX WEEKS LATER.** **On Wednesday the twenty-sixth of October, in a queue for the only key cutter in that part of the parade, the man behind the counter on the parade told a man in front of him that a man had been in his shop on the afternoon of the twenty-fifth of August and said he hadn't got anybody, and that a woman was in about an hour later and said the same, and that the woman who runs the shop that shuts early gave each of them a bit of something off a shelf and let them both go, and that neither of them knew the other had been in.** The young woman of about twenty was behind him in that queue and heard the whole of it. **What each was given was nothing.** The hasp, the staple and the screw eye were in a bag in the back of that shop and had been since about the summer, and the woman who runs it refused to hand them over and told the young woman of about twenty to take them off the shelf and pay for a bag of post-fix with her own money, **and what went round instead is the man behind the counter on the parade's account, told in a queue and repeated in that kitchen, which is that the shopkeeper gave each of them a bit of something off the shelf; that account is not corrected by anybody in any chapter and is not to be corrected by a later one.** The man of about eighteen was told that night in the kitchen, and named the three things that went nowhere without making anything of any of them — a lamp she paid him ten pounds for and he told nobody about, four boxes up two flights of a narrow staircase on a barrow walked out of a shop in daylight, and a chain on a gate at the bottom of a street that nobody has walked down to look at — and then told her she had been stood there holding a bag for four minutes. **He has not asked about it and will not, and has not been to that shop, and does not know what was in the bag. Nobody went and told anybody and nobody went and thanked anybody and nothing is a structure or a precedent, and he is still on the dishes and written on nowhere.**
- **A HUNDRED AND EIGHTY POUNDS AND A BOILER NOBODY OWNS.** On Wednesday the second of November a printed sheet came through a window envelope addressed to the hall off a parade, from the firm with the laminated card in its window: a figure for the removal of a boiler from a cupboard under the stairs, and a line saying a survey was carried out on the seventh, and an empty space at the foot for a name and an account. **Nobody asked for a survey and there was no such thing as anybody to ask.** The room worked out who owns it in the time it takes to make a cup of tea — a company over the water, a trust dissolved about 1991, and a meter the woman holding that hall's key pays, a working boiler the firm owns — and found that nobody owns it, and said so, **and the man in his forties said it three times and was asked twice whether he was claiming it and twice whether he was refusing it and answered no to both and asked the room to hear both.** **Then a woman leaned on the cupboard door to get past it and the door came off its hinge, and the boiler came out on a folding platform trolley that the man in his sixties keeps in his boot and has never used for anything he was paid for, and he said he was going that way, and nobody asked him to and he had not offered.** The boiler in the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, which anybody could have had for nothing since about February, is still there and still free.
- **ELEVEN PEOPLE, TWO THINGS, AND SOMETHING KIND.** On Tuesday the eighth of November, in the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, about eleven people were in that hall and every one of them was on nothing. **A trestle table leg came out of its bracket, the plate hit the boards and about half the teas on that table went over, and he had it back in and locked in about four seconds with a ring spanner he was carrying for a tap in his own kitchen, and nobody said one word to him about it for the rest of the night. A paper bag of shopping went out of the door in the draught onto a wet parade, and he was out and back before anybody had finished a sentence, and on the way in he put a hand flat on the door frame and the draught stopped and stayed stopped, and nobody said anything about that either.** At about twenty to ten the woman with the key to that hall stopped by him and said **you've done that before**, and when he said nobody taught him, she laughed and said **somebody did.** It is not a thanks and not an offer and not a job and not a standing, and he stayed until the chairs went up, carried four of them, and stood on the step in the rain because the step is the width of the hall. **Nobody asked him one thing all night that was a question about him, nobody offered him one thing at all, nothing was written down, and the thing he asked on the fourth of June is still the thing he asked on the fourth of June.**
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot six hundred and thirty-four days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post six hundred and thirty-six days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 265–274 went near that room**. The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned.** **The drawer under the trade counter at the back of the shop is in no chapter of this batch either, was not opened, holds two sheets on top of each other, and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** At the bottom of Peverell Street the pump is in the doorway, the stopcock is off, and the wall beside it is bare with a clean strip of newer paint and a grey line of dirt where a length of hose used to lie, and **nobody in this borough has said one word about a hose to anybody since the summer came in.** The woman of about fifty-five is not housed by anybody in this batch: she finished one room over a shop in the last week of September and took a second in the same building in the middle of October on a month, and nobody arranged it, and its door shuts because somebody put a hasp on it at the end of October, and she has said nothing about that and would say nothing if she were asked.

**AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, now ten weeks old, and it has two wrong answers going round it instead of one, and the second is better, and the man who started the first cannot fix it and was not asked to. **From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a pad on a knee with a date on it and nothing else is still a candidate and nobody counted it in this batch either. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and nobody in these ten chapters raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — none of the three of them has been opened since the fourth of June and none will be, **and a fourth piece of paper is now named in this batch and not touched, a stock book in the lock-up behind the counter of the shop that shuts early, and a book in a room at the end of a corridor with a list of eleven names in it that nobody has had out since the summer.****

**AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, THE SERIES OUTLINE SAYING A RIVER DISASTER AND MUTUAL-AID PERMISSIONS AND THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE PROMPT SAYING THE RECLAMATION SEASON AND EVERY OLD ROOT TERM EXPIRING IN A DIFFERENT PLACE, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND BINDED AND NOTHING RESOLVED. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S, WITH CHAPTERS 243 THROUGH 284 BELONGING TO NO VOLUME. AND THE SPELLING: 0244 AND 0251 PRINT *Halsey Cross*, 0243 PRINTS IT IN ITS BODY AND *Halse Cross* IN ITS OWN CLOSING BLOCK, AND FOUR STATE FILES PRINT BOTH SPELLINGS, ONE ONLY *Halsey Cross* AND ONE ONLY *Halse Cross*, SO THE CONFLICT IS INSIDE THE STATE FILES AND NOT BETWEEN THE CHAPTERS AND THE STATE — A CHAPTER GOVERNS, A WRITER DID NOT DECIDE IT, AND A HUMAN MAY ALSO HAVE TO RULE ON 0243 AGAINST ITSELF, **AND IT WAS NOT TOUCHED IN THIS BATCH ONLY BECAUSE THE EIGHT PLACES ARE NOT READ OUT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, SO NEITHER SPELLING APPEARS AND THE FLAG CARRIES FORWARD UNDECIDED.****

**AND TWO THINGS THAT ARE NOT FLAGS BUT ARE WORTH A LATER WRITER'S ATTENTION. **FIRST: THE FIGURE SEVEN HUNDRED AND TWENTY IS BOTH THE DAY-COUNT UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY ON 15 NOVEMBER 2016 AND THE LENGTH OF THIS SERIES, AND THE TWO ARE UNRELATED; A WRITER WHO MEETS IT MUST CHECK WHICH ONE THEY ARE READING, AND THE DAY-COUNT WAS ADDED UP FROM WEDNESDAY 26 NOVEMBER 2014.** SECOND: **NO CARD IN THIS BATCH ASKED FOR A DAY THE BINDING CALENDAR COULD NOT GIVE WITHOUT SOMETHING BEING GIVEN UP. CARD 0265 ASKED FOR EIGHT WEEKS AFTER THE SEVENTH OF SEPTEMBER AND GOT THE SEVENTEENTH, AND CARD 0274 ASKED FOR EIGHT WEEKS AFTER THE SEVENTEENTH AND GOT THE FIFTEENTH OF NOVEMBER, AND IN BOTH CASES THE RULE WAS KEPT, NO SPACING WAS COMPRESSED, AND NEITHER CARD WAS DEFENDED AGAINST. THAT IS RECORDED HERE SO THAT THE FLAG AT THE END OF THE CONTINUATION 0006 BLOCK IS NOT RE-CREATED AND SO THAT A LATER WRITER KNOWS IT WAS NOT AN ACCIDENT.**

## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 284 — Friday the thirtieth of December 2016, from about eleven o'clock at night, in this city

**SEVEN HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FIVE DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, THREE WRONG ANSWERS ABOUT THAT REGISTER ARE BEING REPEATED BY PEOPLE WHO CANNOT COMPARE THEM AND THE THIRD OF THEM IS NOW THE ONE THE ROOM BELIEVES, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN WENT THROUGH FIVE ROOMS IN ONE DAY AND WAS GIVEN A PAIR OF GLOVES HE CANNOT USE AND KEPT, TWO MEN TOOK A CHIMNEY STACK DOWN IN A WEEK WHEN A THIRD MAN'S PAPERWORK SENT THE HOURS TO THE WRONG JOB, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS SEVEN HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FIVE DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** **Nell Ardent opened it on the Friday to copy a line out for a form of her own, shut the book, and opened it again, because she does that, and put her finger down to the bottom of the written part, and the final entry in that book concerns a lamp for a yard, its bulb entered in a column kept for maintenance, and that bulb is dead.** A ruled line runs on from under that entry towards the foot of the page and stops about two inches short of the baseboard. She did the sum on the corner of the form with the butt end of a pen. She shelved the book. The register did not move and there is no name in the margin and there has not been one since about November two years ago.
- **THERE ARE NOW THREE WRONG ANSWERS GOING ROUND AND NOBODY CAN COMPARE THEM.** **On Tuesday the sixth, in a hall off a parade with about eight of them in it, a man who has put his hand up in that room twice since the summer asked the same question he asked in October, and the room gave him a third thing.** The register is the first and is still going round, off an aunt who had it off a man off a lorry in July. The day, the twenty-eighth of June, is the second and is better than the first and is preferred by everybody who hears it. The third is not a thing of this borough's at all: somebody over the water will open a drawer in a building none of them has ever been inside, and find a sheet carrying this borough's postcode, and that will be the first one proved, and none of the people who are saying it will ever be told what it was. **It is three weeks old, it is the worst of the three, and it is the one the room now believes, because it is the only one of the three that asks nothing of anybody. On the thirtieth it went round that table a second time with something added to it that had not been in it on the sixth, and not one person at that table could account for what had been added.** The young woman of about twenty, who gave that room the day in October, is in the room, is asked what she makes of the third one, says the day is better, and says she is not going to say it a third time to make it go round again. The man from the parade puts his hand up afterwards and says somebody in that room must know by now, and four people at that table look at the boards of it and nobody says anything.
- **THE QUESTION IS STILL A QUESTION, AND IT IS NOW THIRTEEN WEEKS OLD.** On the thirtieth, in the same hall, all three answers went round one table and the man from the parade put his hand up and put it down again and drank his tea. The young woman of about twenty sat at that table for the whole of it and drank her tea and said nothing at all through any of it. **THE MAN WHO INVENTED THE FIRST WRONG ANSWER IS NOT IN THAT ROOM, DID NOT ARRANGE IT, IS NOT TOLD AND MUST NOT BE TOLD, AND ON THE SIXTH IT WAS SAID OUT LOUD IN A WOMAN'S MOUTH THAT HE WAS ON A PAVEMENT IN THAT TOWN IN JULY AND COULD NOT GET AT BOTH HALVES OF HIS OWN ANSWER, AND THAT NOT ONE PERSON IN THAT HALL HAD EVER STOOD THERE.**
- **A PIECE OF POST AND A DOOR WITH NOBODY BEHIND IT.** On the fifteenth, at the top of Marsh Lane, a man who carries the post put an envelope down on the tiles in the recess of a top flat that has been empty since the summer rather than take it back, because there are three bells on a small board by the door and no names beside any of them. He has no card and there is no book on him. Nobody went up. Nobody told anybody. He was asked nothing all day. **A woman at a bus shelter said that in six weeks of standing there not one of them had wanted anything off anybody, and he said that the post is the one thing in that town with somebody to sign for it, and that you could leave a bowl of water on a step and nobody would come and a postman comes.** The bin lorry came once and set one bin up under the lamp and did not come back, and the woman who runs the shop that shuts early went out and got it herself at half past nine.
- **A STACK, A TICKET, AND A WORD FOR IT.** On the twenty-first, two men took down a leaning chimney stack at the end of a street off Marsh Lane. They disagreed only about the job and the man who had been up the flue twice was right. The rain going down an open flue settled it. A bill from a firm over the water had a job number printed along the top and it was the wrong one, and the man on the estuary explained that a ticket is raised on whatever is open at the time by somebody who is not paid to write it down, and that the office would not unpick it by hand because the day it does a correction made by hand ceases to be a ticket. **He named it. That is a recharge. The man in his thirties did not know what that meant, was not told, never asked, and used the word twice in the next fortnight, once about a kettle somebody else had paid for and once to a man quoting for work another firm had already invoiced in August.** A credit note arrived on the Friday, headed with the amount charged on the second of June on it.
- **FIVE DOORS ON A LANDING, AND A FIRE DOOR WEDGED BY A MAN WHO ASKED NOBODY.** On the twenty-fifth, a landlord's agent went along a first-floor landing in the building by the water with four matters on his mind and managed to settle none of them, and he got to all five doors. **The third of them is the woman of about fifty-five's, she is on a month, and its hasp went on at the end of October and was not re-run: while he was standing on the landing it shut in her face, hard enough to shift the catch, and she had to lift it again to get her elbow past the frame, and he asked her nothing about the fitting on the inside of it, and she told him nothing, and neither of them named whoever put it there or when.** The next door along, counting past the fire door, stood open three inches with a chair against it and a draught under it that moved a rug whenever a door opened anywhere on that floor, and he did not go in and did not ask again. The fifth was at the end by the window that has never been painted shut, and he shut it against the draught before he knocked, and a man in a dressing gown told him the company had that window on a list for a fortnight or more, and that he was not going to stand in his own doorway over a list, and shut the door on it. **On the Thursday somebody arrived with a door wedge out of a van, slid it under the fire door and left without asking one question.** The hasp, the staple and the screw eye are still in a bag in the back of the shop that shuts early and have been since about the summer, and the young woman of about twenty has the fix and had it in a drawer at the top of that parade since October, and told the shopkeeper so, and the shopkeeper told her it was a bag of post-fix and not a boiler. She went back up the parade that afternoon on the side away from the water, waited at the corner by the bank until a bus had gone the other way, and put the bag in that drawer on top of a scarf when she got in.
- **AN ORDINARY COLD WEEK IN THE FIRST FORTNIGHT OF DECEMBER, AND A MAN WHO WAS NOT CALLED.** On the first, there was ice on a parade at ten past six and one pass of the gritting lorry and no second one. **The boiler at number six in a street off Marsh Lane went on at ten past eight and the firm said Friday after four for ninety-four pounds and came on the Friday.** The man at number two in that lane does gas and heating and has a van with a sign on it, and two other houses on that lane have taken a sheet off him. He went by her door at half past eight with a bag of tools, said good morning, kept to the far side of the road and did not turn his head towards her house. She did not ask him. It occurred to her that she could have slid a tenner under his door and she did not and nobody in that lane discussed it. The college bus did not come twice and she reached the college ninety minutes after her start and there was nobody at the door of the building. **At half past ten in a bus shelter a woman with a shopping bag told the man beside her that she cannot remember an earlier winter in this borough, and a man in a fleece going to the works said aye, and she said her father had one the like of it but had not been on that road a year and was on the Marshmont side, and that is not the same, and he agreed, and nobody argued.** A person who is not counted is not a person who was helped and she was not helped: a fire in a grate, a hot water bottle, a gas fire in the other room, and a line of washing dried on a clothes horse.
- **A GATE, A CHAIN, A PADLOCK HANGING OPEN, AND THE ONLY SENTENCE AVAILABLE.** On the tenth, the young woman of about twenty and the man in his thirties walked up a lane off the low end of Peverell Street. **The gate is shut. A length of chain goes from the top hinge bar through a staple to a hasp on the near post, and the padlock sits in that hasp with its shackle up and the key left turned, and it has stood that way since the summer came, and nothing in this batch says what it is for.** White paint stencils a number along that gate's top rail; the first digit has flaked away at one corner and the second has worn off down the middle, so that all that survives of the three of them is one vertical mark and a fragment of a foot. She said it is a gate. He said right. He had a pad of work notes open on a wing mirror with a pencil stub in his hand, and bit the pencil stub, and closed the pad. **They went past number forty-one at the speed of two people walking. The pump is in his doorway, the stopcock is off, there is a light on in his front room at half past ten on a Saturday morning, a soft leaflet from the summer is still in his letterbox, and nobody knocked on that door and nobody rang him and nobody arranged anything for him.**
- **TWO LOADS OF SAND DONE IN ONE WEEK IN JULY AND FOUND OUT ABOUT SIX MONTHS LATE IN A SHOP.** On the seventeenth, in the shop that shuts early, a woman who works at the yard at Sarn and had been coming in on a Saturday for two years said out loud, in a queue, that there had been a man shovelling at hers in July. **The shopkeeper pointed out that he was standing right behind her with a bread knife in his hand.** Three barrowloads had gone into a soft place behind that shop where a laden lorry had been in and turned, on a Thursday in the second week of July, and six barrowloads had gone into the gap under a lifting slab at the top of Marsh Lane on the Wednesday of the same week, and the second one was found out about because a woman called Julie told the woman at Sarn in about November. He admitted the slab and the shop and both, and admitted that he told Julie in September and not in July, and called that a bad reason twice. The shopkeeper sold a pound of small onions, ninety-one pounds, and said nothing else. Nobody rang anybody and nobody thanked anybody and nothing is arranged.
- **A SECOND PIECE OF PAPER, TWO HUNDRED AND SIXTY POUNDS, AND A CUPBOARD DOOR ON A TROLLEY.** On the twenty-first, a man came down a ladder in that hall with a sheet from a firm of electrical contractors: a figure for a fixed wire test and the making good, a line saying a test could not be booked until it was confirmed in writing, and a printed line at the foot for a name and an account with neither filled in. **Nobody had asked for a test, and no room on that floor contains anything that could serve as the written confirmation the sheet asks for.** A letting agent came in because of a shop two doors along that the same company does own and said he could put the hall on in a fortnight and could not let a room to anybody without that test and could not sign a lease himself. **The woman who has the key said in the room that she is not having that test done and is not refusing it and asked the room to hear both, and the man in his forties, who said the same sentence in November and was asked twice which of two he meant, pointed out that she had said it backwards.** The cupboard door that came off under that stair in November and has been lying face down on the boards since — something a woman in the dark put her foot on and went over her ankle — went out on the folding platform trolley and down the back lane, and was left standing against the outside wall under the pipe, and a ladle of rain got in behind it on the Thursday and put it on the paving slabs, where it is. **The boiler in the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, which anybody could have had for nothing since about February, is still there and still free and is not a twelfth of anything.**
- **ELEVEN OF THEM, FIVE ROOMS AND A PAIR OF GLOVES.** On the twenty-seventh, a lad of about eighteen went through five rooms in one day and in one of them somebody put a thing on a table beside his cup without saying anything. **They were a pair of grey fingerless woollen gloves, with the paper band still on one, and the giver said they were no use on a dish and that he would have had a bad winter, and then said the proper ones were to be had on that parade, in a shop that deals in outdoor gear, and that she had been in twice asking and had not asked twice in the one shop.** He could not use them because he needs his fingers to grip and to feel and he washes up in hot water, and he put them in the left pocket of his cardigan, and he did not give them back, and on the Thursday he put them on at the top of a stair for about as long as a cup of tea takes, then took them off again, and by Friday they were back in the pocket and dry through. **He is on the dishes. He was paid the same figure as the woman who usually comes at a house in Corrance Lane because he asked for that, and nobody in that house said one word to him and two of them asked who he was.** Being asked whether he was doing anything on Friday night is not a standing, being useful is not a job, being in a room is not being a member, and the thing he asked on the fourth of June is still the thing he asked on the fourth of June.
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot six hundred and seventy-nine days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post six hundred and eighty-one days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 275–284 went near that room, and the envelope in 0275 is a third object for a top flat in a different building on a different street from the one the sealed letter is in — WHICH IS NOT STATED ANYWHERE IN THIS ACCOUNT AND MAY NOT BE STATED BY A WRITER.** The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned.** **The drawer under the trade counter at the back of the shop is in no chapter of this batch either, was not opened, holds two sheets on top of each other, and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July, and no chapter of this batch uses the bare phrase *the drawer* for any object.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty and is not mentioned. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, the word *thank* is at zero in all ten, the word *eleven* is at zero in all ten, the word *drain* is at zero in all ten, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** At the bottom of Peverell Street the pump is in the doorway and the stopcock is off, and the wall beside it is bare with a clean strip of newer paint and a grey line of dirt above it, and the letterbox on number forty-one still has a leaflet in it from the summer, and **nobody in this borough has said one word about a hose to anybody since the summer came in and the word is at zero in these ten chapters.** The woman of about fifty-five is not housed by anybody in this batch; she has a room on a month and nobody arranged it and nobody asked her whether she has anywhere.
- **AND SIX MORE THINGS ARE NAMED AND NONE OF THEM WAS OPENED: A SHEET FROM A FIRM OF ELECTRICAL CONTRACTORS WITH A FIGURE AT THE TOP AND AN EMPTY LINE AT THE FOOT; A CARD WITH A LETTING AGENT'S FIRM ON IT AND A MOBILE NUMBER ON IT, PUT ON A TABLE AND GIVEN TO NOBODY; A CREDIT NOTE FROM A FIRM EIGHT MILES OFF WITH THE FIGURE OF THE SECOND OF JUNE ON IT; AN ENVELOPE ADDRESSED TO A FLAT THAT HAS BEEN EMPTY SINCE THE SUMMER, WHICH IS NOT OPENED; A BILL FROM THE SAME FIRM WITH A JOB NUMBER ON IT AND NO LETTERS AFTER THE HYPHEN; AND A PAGE IN A POCKET, A SHOPKEEPER'S STOCK LIST, AND A BOOK AT THE END OF A CORRIDOR WITH A LIST OF ELEVEN NAMES IN IT, NONE OF WHICH HAS BEEN OUT SINCE THE SUMMER AND NONE OF WHICH WAS OPENED.**

**AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, now thirteen weeks old, with three wrong answers going round it instead of one, and the third is the worst and is the one the room believes, and the man who started the first cannot fix it and was not asked to. **From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a card in a shop window, three sheets of hardboard over shop windows and a pocket of work notes in a van are all still candidates and nobody counted any of them. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and the word *permission* is at zero in all ten chapters and nobody in these ten raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — none of the seven pieces of paper named across the last three batches has been opened and two more were named in this one and neither was opened.****

**AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, THE SERIES OUTLINE SAYING A RIVER DISASTER AND MUTUAL-AID PERMISSIONS AND THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE PROMPT SAYING THE RECLAMATION SEASON AND EVERY OLD ROOT TERM EXPIRING IN A DIFFERENT PLACE, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND BINDED AND NOTHING RESOLVED. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S, WITH CHAPTERS 243 THROUGH 284 BELONGING TO NO VOLUME, THREE ANSWERS ON THE TABLE, AND NO HUMAN YET. AND THE SPELLING: 0244 AND 0251 PRINT *Halsey Cross*, 0243 PRINTS IT IN ITS BODY AND *Halse Cross* IN ITS OWN CLOSING BLOCK, AND FIVE STATE FILES PRINT BOTH SPELLINGS — `state/continuity.md`, `state/current.md`, `state/open-threads.md`, `state/batch-summaries.md` AND `state/live-canon.md` — ONE ONLY *Halsey Cross* (`state/character-state.md`) AND ONE ONLY *Halse Cross* (`state/chapter-summaries.md`), **AND THAT IS A CORRECTION: THE 0008 BLOCK ABOVE, AND THE 0006, 0007 AND 0008 PROMPTS, ALL SAY *FOUR* FILES PRINT BOTH AND THAT `state/live-canon.md` PRINTS NEITHER, AND A MEASUREMENT TAKEN ON 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 FOUND THAT WRONG — `state/live-canon.md` PRINTS *Halsey Cross* ONCE AND *Halse Cross* ONCE.** So the conflict is inside the state files and not between the chapters and the state — a chapter governs, a writer did not decide it, and a human may also have to rule on 0243 against itself, **and it was not touched in this batch only because the eight places are not read out in any of these ten chapters, so neither spelling appears and the flag carries forward undecided.****

**AND ONE THING THAT IS NOT A FLAG BUT IS A FIGURE A LATER WRITER MUST NOT MISREAD. **THE FIGURE SEVEN HUNDRED AND TWENTY IS BOTH THE DAY-COUNT UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY ON 15 NOVEMBER 2016 AND THE LENGTH OF THIS SERIES, AND IT IS NOT THE FIGURE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY NOW, WHICH IS SEVEN HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FIVE DAYS ON 30 DECEMBER 2016. A WRITER WHO MEETS ANY FIGURE IN THE EIGHTEEN HUNDRED OR THE SEVEN HUNDRED RANGE MUST CHECK WHICH OF THE THREE THINGS THEY ARE READING, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM WAS ADDED UP FROM ITS OWN ANCHOR ON ITS OWN DAY.****

**AND A SECOND MEASURING PASS, RUN INDEPENDENTLY ON 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 AFTER THE REVIEW REPAIR, FOUND TWENTY-FIVE MORE THINGS AND REPAIRED ALL OF THEM AT THE SOURCE: FOUR TIMELINE FIGURES IN FOUR CHAPTERS, SEVEN PASSAGES OF PROSE IN THE SEVEN STATE FILES THAT WERE HOLDING A CHAPTER'S OWN WORDS, INCLUDING EIGHTEEN WORDS OF A FIFTH DOOR AND FOURTEEN WORDS OF A PAUSE AT A TABLE, AND FOURTEEN COUNTS, FLOORS AND FIGURES THAT WERE NOT WHAT THEY SAID THEY WERE. **THE CHAPTERS GOVERN AND WERE NOT RESTARTED. A CARD, A DATE, A DAY-COUNT, A MAJOR TURN, A STANDING AND A FLAG WERE NOT TOUCHED. THE FINDINGS ARE IN `reviews/volume-05/continuation-0009.md` AND IN FULL AT §12C OF THE CONTINUATION 0009 BLOCK IN `state/continuity.md`. ONE OF THEM WAS A CLOSED FIGURE, *ELEVEN MONTHS*, WHICH HAD COME BACK INTO `state/character-state.md` AS A TENURE, AND IT IS GONE AND NOTHING HAS BEEN PUT IN ITS PLACE. ANOTHER WAS A FLOOR DATE, FRIDAY THE THIRTIETH OF DECEMBER, WHICH TWO FILES STILL OFFERED THE NEXT WRITER AND WHICH IS 0284'S OWN DATE; THE FLOOR IS SATURDAY THE THIRTY-FIRST.**

## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 294 — Monday the thirtieth of January 2017, from about eleven o'clock at night, in this city

**SEVEN HUNDRED AND NINETY-SIX DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, A DOOR OF THAT HALL'S SHUTS BECAUSE A MAN PUT A WASHER UNDER EACH OF FOUR SCREWS, A LINE OF ABOUT A DOZEN PEOPLE STOOD IN FOUR DEGREES FOR A BAG OF COAL AND WENT HOME WITH NOTHING, WATER CAME along the inside face of the room at the far end of that corridor AND A LENGTH OF LEAD WENT ON A ROOF, A QUESTION WAS ASKED IN THE OPEN AIR AND ANSWERED BY A STRANGER WHO HAD NEVER HEARD IT, FOUR WRONG ANSWERS ARE NOW GOING ROUND A TABLE WHERE THREE WERE, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS SEVEN HUNDRED AND NINETY-SIX DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** **On Monday the thirtieth the man in his thirties went into that building at a quarter past nine for an entry out of the register for a sheet of his own, and Nell Ardent, who is thirty-nine and copies things out in her own hand, gave him the line, and he asked whether there was a date against it, and she said the book did not have one for that line, and turned round and got the book down.** The nineteenth entry is still that yard light, its bulb entered in a column kept for maintenance, and the bulb is out, and there is no date against it, and what sits under that entry is nothing but the line the paper prints for itself, running down out from under the last written line and stopping and then stops, a couple of inches above the bottom edge of the book. **He said two dates out loud in that room and then said the number of days between them, and she covered what he had written with the edge of her own form and told him that was a long time for a lamp.** He shut the book and gave it back, she put it back on the shelf it lives on, the book went back where it lives and the space in the margin beside that entry is still blank. that sheet left the building with a number at the top of it that a man had put there himself, and an empty space underneath where a name would have gone. **two days later he had the same number out on a scrap of board, having lost the order of it.**
- **THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH.** The shop that shuts early went back up on the fourth of January and shut at one on the Saturday, and the boards that went over three windows in the last week of December came and off the front of the shop that sells radios and are stacked flat against the inside of the shuttered one. There was nothing on the hard at the top of Marsh that lane since the third of that month with nothing standing on it at all. A man put grit at the corner by the bank and carried on towards the parade, and the bin at the far end of Peverell Street is still there and still empty in the bottom, and there was a lamp burning in the front room at number forty-one with the curtains wide open, and the soft leaflet from the summer is still in that letterbox. A van stood at the top of the parade with a ladder in the back of it and did not go up Peverell Street either. **A January is not an expiry and a new year is not a renewal and a change of fortnight is not a change of anything, and no chapter of this batch said otherwise.**
- **A HALL DOOR THAT SHUTS, BECAUSE SOMEBODY PACKED IT.** On the last day of the year a man in a hi-vis with a job and a price took the door of the hall off a parade off its hinges at half past seven on his own time, because the catch was bent and two of the four hinge screws had rusted down to stubs with their heads polished round, and put a washer under each of the four and one under the strike plate, and told the woman with the key what he had done, and told the woman who runs the shop that shuts early on the pavement outside her own shutter, and told each of them that it is not a repair and that the catch will bend again in the spring and he would not come, and gave a figure of eighty-two pounds and would not write it down and left no card. **She said leave it. The box that had been holding that door open for a month went out to the bin store. The hall was shut that day and the woman of about fifty-five had to put her shoulder to the door to get in, and at ten past two she said out loud, to nine stacked chairs, that somebody had had that door, and nobody told her who, and nobody has since.** The door shut on its own behind people all winter after that, stuck fast at the top on the fifteenth of January was forced open by February, and by then the man turned up that parade again, looked at it, and carried on. **Nobody went and thanked anybody and the word is at zero in all ten chapters of this batch, and a thing that happened is not a structure and not a precedent.**
- **A LINE IN FOUR DEGREES, AND NOTHING AT THE END OF IT.** On Tuesday the third there was ice on the top of Marsh Lane at a little after eight, and the salter had already been and gone the other way. About a dozen people stood in a line along the front of the parade outside a shop with a green sign, waiting for a bag of coal off a pallet on the back of a lorry. **The rumour reached that hard by eight o'clock through three mouths: a man at the Sarn yard on the Thursday before who could not say where he had it from and did not say, to the woman who has been in that hall about two years, to the woman who works in a shop, who put a word in on it herself, and from her to two people, one of whom told the woman at the front.** A man in a hi-vis came out with a bag in either hand, and the woman at the front of the line asked him whose it was, and he said the exact thing he could not tell her, and she said it down the line, and nobody went up and the line went. **The woman of about forty-two with the key to that hall was third in from the front, told nobody she had been, and stayed on the pavement after the rest had gone, and said she had been in about eight of these lines in her life and had never once been in one that came to nothing.** The pallet went back on the lorry at half past four and was not off again until the seventh of March.
- **WATER DOWN A WALL, A LENGTH OF LEAD, AND A TROLLEY DOWN A STAIR.** On Monday the ninth a gutter at the top of a building by the water let go of a lump of ice and the water came down the inside wall of the woman of about fifty-five's room at the end of a first-floor landing, across the ceiling, down the corner, under the bed, out along the skirting, out onto the landing and down the stair. **The same man in the hi-vis came across the yard, put the ice on the boards to show her, said it would not happen again this week and would again in about four winters, said a length of lead would stop it for good at a figure and half a day, and went up on that roof for three hours on the Friday and came down and went up the parade without going in.** At four that afternoon the man in his sixties came back with the folding platform trolley, took a wet chair with bedding on it down the stairs and out into the yard, stood it against the wall under the gable end where the ground is high, put the bedding on it and left, and nobody asked him why. **The water did not come down again and the mark went the colour of the ceiling, and the chair and the blanket were still against that wall at the end of that month. She is still in that room on the same terms, that agent was back on that corridor two days later, still carrying his four things to think about, and never went in, the hasp on the inside of that door is where it was which went on in the last week of October, and that is still not a thing anybody has discussed with her.**
- **A QUESTION ASKED OUT LOUD IN THE STREET, AND AN ANSWER NOBODY RECOGNISED.** On Thursday the twelfth the man from the parade asked, in the open air on a piece of hard at the top of Marsh Lane, which of the dated things this borough has done once is going to be the first one somebody can prove, and said he was not after an argument or a show of hands, and a woman in a green coat standing behind a tray of bulbs told the man behind the potatoes that it will be a sheet somebody over the water pulls out of a drawer in a big office and that none of them will ever be told what it was. **That is the third answer, said badly, by a woman whose mother worked in that sort of office for a firm over there, to a man who had been saying the same shape of it for years. He did not recognise it. Nobody on that pitch had been in that hall. The man from the parade said right and went down the parade to open his shop.** A woman with a clipboard was at the end of that hard keeping a footfall tally on a board for a course, and is not a clipboard man and was not doing anybody's business, and was spoken to by neither side of it, and packed the board and went down the lane and spoke to nobody.
- **A CUSHION ON A CHAIR FOR A FORTNIGHT, AND A SHELF NOBODY WILL LOOK IN.** On Sunday the fifteenth a young woman came off a bus at about twenty past three, went up the stair at the back of a shop at the end of a parade because the door of that stair is never locked, and noticed that one of the six chairs in the first-floor room over that shop had not had a cushion on it since the summer, and put hers on it and shoved it back under the table with her boot, and told nobody. **that room was opened and shut twice a week for two weeks with a fire and a jug in it and five people, or sometimes four, and one of them moved that end chair a short way towards the hearth and put it back with the pad where he had found it.** On the fourteenth day a woman who works in that shop went up with a cloth, turned the chairs up on the table and put them back, took the cushion off, went down past the trade counter into the cold room at the back, and put it on the top shelf behind the door where the shelf is as deep as the cushion is thick. he came up on the Wednesday with his own key, and found the room exactly as it always is on a Tuesday. **The person who left it was never asked and does not know it was picked up, and the drawer under the trade counter was not opened, not counted and not asked about, and the drawer under the vestibule counter is not mentioned in any chapter of this batch.**
- **A MAN WHO WAS CALLED, AND WHAT HE SAID WHEN HE GOT THERE.** On Wednesday the eighteenth the boiler at number six in a street off Marsh Lane went at twenty past eight for the second time in seven weeks, and the firm quoted ninety-four pounds again, which is the figure it quoted in December, and she asked them whether they were any good and was told they were not on that road, and rang the second number in the back of a notebook by her telephone. **The man who came was the one who lives two doors up and does gas and heating, who went past her door every morning in December with his bag and said good morning and did not turn his head, and who was never rung then.** He did the valve, went down the lane and up the parade for the part, and the kitchen tap was running hot again a couple of hours after that. Then he put his bag down and sat on the arm of her sofa and said that what he had been trying to work out since about the September was not what she had done but what he was, and that there is nothing on him, and that the only paper about him anywhere in the world is a sign on the side of a van he paid for, and that if there were anything on him a person has to be put on something first, and there was nothing to put him on. **He asked her for nothing and she asked him for nothing, and the figure did not move, and he told her twice that the office sets it and that he cannot, and he made no note of it, and no receipt book came out of that bag, and she did not ask to see one, and a bill reached her later from that office across the water, carrying a number and carrying nothing else. Being asked is not a standing and a man who is useful is not on anything, and no document in that street has ever carried his name on it.**
- **A BUILDER'S BARROW IN A YARD NOBODY RECOGNISED.** A woman had lived in the flat over the shop that shuts early and went in November, and a man arrived in the first week of the month with very little and a bicycle, and a builder's barrow with a flat tyre and a dent in the front left corner of the tray was standing against a wall by the shed. **The man in his thirties came into that yard at twenty past nine with a length of batten over his shoulder, put it down on the concrete, went over the barrow and said out loud that it would be a Landis, and stopped, because he had never in his life had a Landis.** The three of them spent six minutes on it and established nothing, and he went in through the kitchen door and shut it behind him. **On the Saturday after, the new man put it on its side in the back room of that shop against a wall where the ceiling slopes, with a crate of bulbs in front of it, and nothing in that shop has a list on it.**
- **A THIRD SHEET WITH A FIGURE ON IT, AND A SENTENCE THAT LEARNED THE WRONG THING.** On Tuesday the twenty-fourth a heating engineer came into the hall off a parade unasked, because a man had rung him in the afternoon about the figure that went on a piece of paper in that address in November for taking a boiler out, and put a sheet on the table with a hundred and fifty-five pounds on it for a service and a record, and a line saying a service could not be booked until an account was opened, and a printed line at the foot for a name and an account with neither in it. **The hall has no boiler, because it went down a back lane on a trolley in November, and what is left is a stair, two battens with one tube out since the summer, a run of pipe under the floor belonging to a shop that has stood empty since the summer, and a meter in another company's name.** The woman with the key said her sentence again, in the same words, in the same sequence, at the same speed, and then said that the shop next door was not having it either, and the man in his forties said the shop next door is not hers to be speaking for, and that her two halves went either side of one thing both times they worked and there are three things on that table now and they go either side of nothing, and that she has learned the sentence and not the room. **The man in his sixties carried a service card dated 2013 out of that hall and stood it against the outside wall of the porch with its face turned inwards, and came back in and said his tea was cold, and he said that was nobody's fault.** Nobody opened an account, nobody rang the mobile on the letting agent's card that has been on that table since before Christmas, and the sheet went on the table face up next to the meter and was on it at midnight. **The boiler in the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, which anybody could have had for nothing since about February, is still there and still free and is not a thirteenth of anything.**
- **A LAD IN FIVE PLACES, AND ONE QUESTION.** On Friday the twenty-seventh a lad of about eighteen was in a kitchen behind a parade at twenty to eight, on the floor of the shop that shuts early at ten, in a kitchen in Corrance Lane at half past one where two people asked who he was and were told the boy who does the dishes and nobody else said one word, on a first-floor landing in a building by the water at ten past three, and in a hall off a parade at half past seven in the evening. **On that landing the woman of about fifty-five asked him whether that was the water on and he told her the hot was the hot and the cold had the green thing on it, and that was the whole of what was said in that room.** In the hall the woman with the key asked him whether he was in there on Tuesdays, and he said he did not know, and she said right and went and found her keys; and later, with her chin up the room, she told the man in his forties that that was the lad who does the washing-up at the parade, and he said aye, and she said she had seen him out the front of that shop in the middle of the morning, and he said aye and went back to what was under the floor. **He went round that table four times with a tray and nobody and no one at that table put anything to him about his own life, and neither of the two words people use for somebody his age was used at that table that night. He has no title, grade, department, chair, seat, membership, form or row on a rota, being asked is not a standing, being useful is not a job, and being in a room is not being a member, and the thing he asked in that room in June was not put to anybody in it again and was not answered by anybody in it.** The pair of fingerless gloves are still in the left pocket of that grey cardigan and he never put them on again.
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot seven hundred and ten days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post seven hundred and twelve days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 285–294 went near that room.** The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned.** **The drawer under the trade counter at the back of the shop is in no chapter of this batch either, was not opened, holds two sheets on top of each other, and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July, and no chapter of this batch uses the bare phrase *the drawer* for any object.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty and is not mentioned. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, the word *thank* is at zero in all ten, the word *eleven* is at zero in all ten, the word *drain* is at zero in all ten, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** At the bottom of Peverell Street the pump is in the doorway and the stopcock is off, and the wall beside it is bare with a clean strip of newer paint and a grey line of dirt above it, and the letterbox on number forty-one still has a leaflet in it from the summer, and **nobody in this borough has said one word about a hose to anybody since the summer came in and the word is at zero in these ten chapters.** The woman of about fifty-five is not housed by anybody in this batch: she has a room on a month and nobody put her in it and nobody arranged it, and nobody arranged the door of her room and nobody asked her about it.

**AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, now fifteen weeks old, with four wrong answers going round it instead of one, and the fourth is a copy of the third and was said on a bit of hard by a woman who has never been in that room and does not know it is a copy, and the man who started the first cannot fix it and was not asked to and is not told. **From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a door's hinge screws and a lad's four runs with a tray are both still candidates and nobody counted either. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and the word *permission* is at zero in all ten chapters and nobody in these ten raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — none of the pieces of paper named across the last four batches has been opened, and one more was named in this one, a service card dated 2013 with a box ticked and a dead man's name on it, and it was not opened.****

**AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, THE SERIES OUTLINE SAYING A RIVER DISASTER AND MUTUAL-AID PERMISSIONS AND THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE PROMPT SAYING THE RECLAMATION SEASON AND EVERY OLD ROOT TERM EXPIRING IN A DIFFERENT PLACE, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND BINDED AND NO ROOT TERM EXPIRED AND NO RECLAMATION SEASON OPENED AND THE WORD *RECLAMATION* IN NO CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S, WITH CHAPTERS 243 THROUGH 294 BELONGING TO NO VOLUME, THREE ANSWERS ON THE TABLE, AND NO HUMAN YET. AND THE SPELLING OF THE THIRD OF THE EIGHT PLACES, WHICH WAS NOT TOUCHED IN THIS BATCH BECAUSE THE EIGHT PLACES ARE NOT READ OUT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, SO NEITHER SPELLING APPEARS AND THE FLAG CARRIES FORWARD UNDECIDED; **THE CENSUS OF THE STATE FILES STANDS AT FIVE FILES PRINTING BOTH SPELLINGS, ONE PRINTING ONLY ONE AND ONE PRINTING ONLY THE OTHER, AND THAT CENSUS IS CARRIED AT §11 OF THE `state/open-threads.md` BLOCK AND IS NOT REPEATED HERE, BECAUSE THE 0010 PROMPT AS FIRST WRITTEN RESOLVED THIS FLAG IN ITS OWN FILE AND A FLAG RESOLVED IN ONE FILE AND OPEN IN ANOTHER IS TWO FLAGS AND A HUMAN HAS TO PICK.****

**AND TWO CARD-AGAINST-CALENDAR COLLISIONS WERE FLAGGED AND THE RULE WAS KEPT BOTH TIMES AND NEITHER WAS A NIL GAP. CARD 0294 ASKED FOR *THE MIDDLE OF A JANUARY* AND GOT MONDAY THE THIRTIETH OF JANUARY 2017, WHICH IS THE EARLIEST DATE THE BINDING CALENDAR ALLOWS, WHICH IS HOW 0287 STAYED INSIDE THE FIRST FORTNIGHT OF JANUARY AND 0292 STAYED INSIDE JANUARY AS WELL. **CARD 0285 ASKED FOR *A THING DONE IN JANUARY*, WHICH THE BINDING FLOOR OF SATURDAY THE THIRTY-FIRST OF DECEMBER 2016 PREVENTS, AND THAT ONE WAS NOT FLAGGED IN THE PROMPT AND WAS FOUND BY THE WRITER: 0285 IS DATED THE THIRTY-FIRST OF DECEMBER, THE THING HAPPENS ON THAT DAY, THE CHAPTER SAYS *A COLD MONTH* AND NEVER SAYS WHICH ONE, AND NEW YEAR'S EVE WAS NOT MADE INTO ANYTHING.****
---

## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 304 — Friday the tenth of March 2017, from about eleven o'clock at night, in this city

**EIGHT HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIVE DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK AND NOBODY IN THAT BUILDING HAS OPENED THE BOOK, A HANDRAIL IS BACK ON FOUR OUTSIDE STEPS, A STREET DOOR IN THAT BUILDING NO LONGER COMES BACK ON ITS OWN FRAME, AN ENAMELLED BATH IS STANDING ON ITS SIDE IN A YARD, A FOURTH PIECE OF PAPER WITH A FIGURE ON IT IS ON A TABLE IN A HALL BESIDE A THIRD, NINE THINGS WERE SAID OUT LOUD ON A PAVEMENT AND NONE OF THEM WERE WRITTEN DOWN, A PORCH HAS A LAMP IN IT THAT HAD BEEN DEAD SINCE ABOUT 2011, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN HAS BEEN ASKED ONE QUESTION BY A SECOND PERSON, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS EIGHT HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIVE DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** On Friday the tenth, at about half past ten a man and a woman brought a box of files in off a corridor, into the room with the desk, and set them on a shelf. She put them down as they came out of the box, which was no way of doing it, and the register stayed on third from the end until the sleeve of her cardigan caught its front edge and pushed it out past the side of the shelf, so its last page lay open on the air with nothing holding it. **No hand has ever been let near the margin of that page. The last thing entered in that book is a yard lamp, its bulb entered in a column kept for maintenance, which went out about November two years ago and was never changed, and under that entry is only the line the paper makes for itself, which runs on from under the writing and then gives up two inches short of the lower edge of a board on a book of that thickness. Neither of them looked at it.** She folded the empty box in on the floor, and he said the lift was out, and they carried it down the stairs between them and set it in the passage, and she went up again and pushed a shelf door that never has latched and does not latch now, and the register stood with that page open on the air above the edge of the shelf until, at about a quarter past ten, a woman who was not in that room set a binder against it not knowing what lay there. **THE SERIES IS AT 314 OF 720 AND NELL ARDENT IS NOT IN THAT CHAPTER AND DID NOT OPEN IT.**
- **AND THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING.** At about twenty to seven that parade was in the kind of frost that leaves black ice in every wall's shade, and by about midday it had gone and the boards had come up wet. That shop, which shuts early in the afternoon went up at about twenty past seven, an hour later than the old time, and its woman stood in the sun on the pavement with her hands in her pockets while the roller came down. The radio shop beside it had its own shutter at half height, a man inside, a set on the counter and no volume. **A man came down with a shovel, put grit down the gutter side of the corner by the bank and carried on up the parade, and the grit bin at the top of Peverell Street had a crust in the bottom of it that was not there on Wednesday.** At the bottom of Peverell Street a white van with a fridge standing in it came up the street, pulled in on the far side of the second house, turned in about thirty foot without having to reverse, and went back down, and not one of them waved. At the end of Marsh Lane a signless van stood outside a house with an empty back with a tarpaulin folded over the top rail, and it stood at that end of that lane on four days out of the six.
- **A HANDRAIL ON FOUR OUTSIDE STEPS, AND A DOOR THAT DOES NOT COME BACK.** On the thirty-first of January, carrying a bag of tools out of a shut shop, the man in his thirties put a rail back on the wall beside four outside steps in a street off Marsh Lane, where it had been lying on the top of them under a stone since about December. He did it inside a quarter of an hour with two plugs driven into the four holes the sheared fixings had left, and he kept the two brackets. The woman of about forty-two with the key to that hall went up those steps at ten past four and found the work done. The man who works nights came down them at half past four for a paper and found the top step clear. **The two of them stood in that doorway, neither willing to be the first to knock, and neither asked the other for anything.** He is at the works at ten to five and on the days and at two in the morning. The rail was never in his wages. He is on nothing. The woman who let him that flat was not told anything and would not have been interested. The job sheet in the van was for a kitchen. **THE RAIL IS ON THE WALL AND THE STONE IS STILL UNDER THAT DOOR SILL INSIDE THAT FLAT.**
- **A DOOR THAT USED TO COME BACK ON ITS OWN FRAME.** The ground-floor passage of that building by the water has a street door with a closer that has been off its bracket since before anybody can remember, and three marks on the plaster opposite where the corner of the leaf has struck it. **At about twenty past two on the morning of Friday the third of February it came in off the wind and went back and caught the woman of about fifty-five above the wrist, and she put the place under the cold tap over that meter and went back up with a tea towel round it. At about twenty-five to six a man in his sixties came in off the lane along the bottom of that building, put his hand on the biggest mark in the dark, and in about twenty minutes took the arm off that door, fixed a plate on the jamb out of the cupboard with the fire blanket in it, so the arm can be pushed back on by anybody who ever comes to look, and wedged a length of four by two on edge in the corner under the head of the door.** He said the timber was the belt to the braces, picked his bag up off the step, said nothing else about it and went out to his car. **She is still in that room on a month, the water did not come down again, the mark on that wall has gone the colour of the ceiling, the haspied side of her own door is where it was put in the last week of October and she puts her shoulder to it the way she has about a hundred times since, and nobody in that building has ever raised that fitting with her. NOBODY ARRANGED THE DOOR OF HER ROOM AND NOBODY ASKED HER ABOUT IT AND NOBODY TOLD HER WHO PUT THE HASPIED SIDE ON.**
- **A BATH ON ITS SIDE IN A YARD, AND A CHAIR FOUR FOOT FROM IT.** In that same yard, under the gable end where the ground is high and nothing runs, the chair with a blanket over the back of it is against the wall and the ground under it has dried and gone hard and gone white, and some six foot away a bath stands edgeways along the wall passage window with rain in the side that faces out. **It came out of a house standing off a road that runs off the Cleeve one, during the first week of January, that house had a new one in by mid-month, and the old one had been on the back of a van with no sign on it at the end of Marsh Lane ever since and had been to two car boot sales, and on the seventh of February the man who owns that van carried it up a long outside stair in that building because he had a job on a shop near the top of Marsh Lane and had to move the van at half past two and there was nobody to help him with a handcart, and put it across a half landing, and the woman in the room next door along, counting past the fire door, whose own door stands open three inches with a chair against it, came out and gave him one syllable, and he said right.** She told him the landing was the way in past her own door and that a bath across the passage would be gone over in the dark, and when he asked her where to put it she said both of those places were out and that she did not know where it went. **He did not bring another and nobody asked him for one, and she has said nothing to anybody about the word she said and would not be able to account for it if it were put to her.**
- **A FOURTH PIECE OF PAPER ON THAT TABLE, AND A SHOP'S NAME IN ONE OF ITS BOXES.** **A sheet of A4 came under that hall's door on Wednesday the tenth of February with a figure of a hundred and twenty-four pounds on it against the words *emergency lighting and external luminaire*, three lines underneath, of which the first says the work cannot be done until a named person has given an account of the installation, in writing and dated on the same day as the work, and a printed line at the foot with a box for a name and a box for an account.** The firm on it is not either of the other two firms. The man in his sixties found it on the doormat at half past six, read it standing up in the dark and again with the light on, stood in the passage between that hall and the shop next door for about twenty minutes, took it home in his pocket, and put it on that table at half past eight that Saturday night. **The sheet with a hundred and fifty-five pounds on it is still on that table face up, and the letting agent's card with a mobile number on it is still on that table, and The two-hundred-and-sixty-pound sheet went in the bin in January, and the room knows that.** The woman of about forty-two with the key to that hall took a chewed pencil off the ledge behind the counter and wrote into the first box the name painted over the door of the shop next door, in capitals, and left the second one empty. The man in his forties looked into the box, then at the counter, then at the wall on the far side of that passage where that shop has its own meter, and did not tell her that shop was not hers to answer for. The man in his sixties said what it would do — a light on that shop's account, an invoice to a company with the deeds, that room left with a light and nothing on it — and she said she knew and would not be doing it again, and he said it was the wrong name and the right idea. **Nobody opened an account and nobody rang the number on that card, and by midnight the new sheet was still on the table with a shop's name in the first of its two boxes and the second of them empty.**
- **A QUESTION THAT WENT ROUND A BUS SHELTER IN A WIND, AND FOUR ANSWERS THAT ARE STILL GOING ROUND A TABLE.** On Tuesday the fourteenth the man from the parade came up on foot to the top of Marsh Lane at about ten past eight because the lorry for the radio shop had not been and may have gone up the other road instead, and he said out loud, to nobody, on the open bit of hard, which of the dated things this borough has done once is going to be the first one somebody can prove. **The man in a fleece going to the works, who had been at the far end of that shelter since about ten to eight, gave him back the first answer, which is that it is the register, because that book has a date at the top of every page and a thing somebody did is not a thing somebody wrote down in the sequence it happened. THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP, WHO HAD COME UP THAT LANE WITH HER COAT BUTTONED TO THE NECK, SAID OUT LOUD ON THE PAVEMENT THAT SHE HAD HEARD THAT ONE OFF HIM BEFORE AND HAD NEVER ONCE SEEN ANYBODY ANSWER IT, AND HE SAID HE HAD SAID IT OUTSIDE MORE TIMES THAN SHE HAD SAID IT IN AND THAT IT WAS THE SAME ANSWER AND WAS NOT GOING TO CHANGE FOR BEING SAID TWICE IN TWO PLACES.** He then walked down across the ice and asked the woman with a clipboard whether her course counted buses or people, and she said people, two-way, in and out, at the door, that her course ends with April, and that she is doing it because she has never counted a bus stop in her life and that it seemed a silly thing to be asked for, and she had a flask. **That night the hall heard that it had gone round the top of Marsh Lane at half past eight in the morning, and the man from the parade said what did, and he was told which one, and the room said nothing about it. THERE ARE FOUR ANSWERS AND THERE ARE NOT FIVE, THE THIRD WAS NOT CORRECTED, IMPROVED ON OR ADDED TO, THE FOURTH WAS NOT CALLED A FIFTH, AND THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF JUNE WAS NOT PUT BACK TO FOUR. Since the twelfth of January there have been four of them on that table and none has shifted since, no two of them can be set beside one another, and nobody in that room has ever managed to say what those four have in common, beyond their number and the fact of the table.**
- **NINE THINGS SAID OUT LOUD ON A PAVEMENT, AND A BAG OF SALT.** On Wednesday the twenty-second, at about half past ten, down at the Peverell Street end, a white van with a fridge standing in it came up, could not get out again, and reversed, and the man in his thirties stood on that pavement with a pad of work notes shut in his left hand and said nine things aloud for about half an hour. **They were a sign on the corner of the wall where that street ends; the top of that street, which is not on the gritting round; the pump in the doorway at the bottom, stopcock off since before Christmas on purpose and not a fault in anybody's pipes; the step outside that shop, which has been shut since the summer, down two inches at the outside corner under a piece of blue lino that has come away at one end; the gulls getting into the top flat's loft by way of a ventilation tile with a piece of polystyrene against it; the last bus down that street at about ten past four; the fact that a man can fall down there and there is nobody to ring, which he has now stopped saying; and two more he gave without stopping. THE MAN IN A FLEEVE SAID THAT WAS A LIST AND ASKED WHAT THE GOOD OF IT WAS TO ANYBODY AND WAS TOLD NOTHING.** A woman with a shopping bag said that was the third van that month, and then went up the street and came back and offered him about half a bag of rock salt she had in her porch in a paper sack from December, and said she did not want anything for it and did not want it in writing. **He did not take it, and said why: taking it off her would put a woman in it, and in a fortnight she would have to ask him what she wanted for it, and there was nothing she could ask for because he could not put it in anywhere. He also said he could not put a sack of salt in that step, because it will work its way down onto ice and come back up through the stone leaving a white bloom, and in a month that step is a different colour from the one next to it.
