SHE SAID RIGHT, WENT ON UP THE STREET, AND NEITHER RETURNED WITH THE SACK NOR MENTIONED THE SALT AGAIN, AND THE JUG IS STILL IN HER PORCH.** The pad was not opened once between half past ten and twenty to twelve. **On the Thursday, working beside a man in Corrance Lane, he put one of the nine into the air again. The man asked which one. He could not recall the run of them, and he put nothing on paper even then. THE LIST IS ON NOTHING.**
- **A CHEST OF HINGES IN A SHED, AND A BINDER ON A SHELF.** On Sunday the nineteenth, at about a quarter to ten, the woman who works in a shop went out into the yard behind that shop because the back door has had a barrel in it since the summer, and by now the barrel is about halfway round, and she had a locksmith's card in her pocket that she has been carrying since the second week of November. **She went into a shed looking for a tin of oddments and no tin was there, and no tin ever had been, and behind the shed, under a sheet of dpm that had come off in the wind and was folded over the alley fence, there was a wooden chest about two foot by three, divided down the middle, with a rope handle at each end, with about four inches of assorted galvanised hinges in it and about nine loose screws in a tobacco tin, and it had been put there by the man in his thirties in the last week of November when he did an over-door.** She had the lid off before she had decided to have it off, and the barrel she came for was on the shelf over the desk in the office with a bit of pencil on that shelf reading *back door* in a hand not hers, and she changed the lock herself, with a screwdriver, inside ten minutes from half past twelve. **The chest went into the shed, along the left-hand wall below the shelf, divided end facing the door, a milk crate in front of it, that door drawn to. A second person works in that shop on weekdays and was not there that Sunday. ON THE THURSDAY THE MAN IN took nine feet of capping into that yard at about ten past ten, AND WENT ROUND THE BACK OF THE SHED FOR BATTEN HE KNEW HE HAD PUT THERE HIMSELF, AND THERE WAS NO BATTEN AND NO DPM OVER ANYTHING AND NO CRATE, AND SOMEBODY HAD DRIVEN THE LEAF ON THE SHED FLOOR INTO A CORNER WITH A BOOT. HE ASKED AT THE FRONT WHETHER THAT SHOP HAD EVER HAD ANY BATTEN AT THE BACK AND WAS TOLD IT HAD NOT HAD ANYTHING AT THE BACK IN HIS LIFE, AND HE SAID RIGHT and came back a week later with batten of his own, at his own cost.**
- **A LAMP IN A PORCH THAT HAD BEEN DEAD SINCE ABOUT 2011, AND NOBODY EITHER WAY.** On Saturday the twenty-fifth at about half past six in the morning, a man in a hi-vis was at the front of the shop next to that hall with a set of keys belonging to a man four miles off, because that shop's landlord is in a town sixty miles off and does not come, and he has walked that parade four times since Christmas. **There is a bulkhead over the porch of that hall that has been dead since about 2011 and a switch inside that porch, on the wall beside the door, which has never done anything because it never had anything behind it. He put a ladder on the top step, had the old lamp out in about four minutes, found about a foot of dead stub above that switch, cut it off, FED THE FLEX BACK UP INTO THE ROSE AND INTO THE SWITCH, pressed the switch with his thumb, and that porch was lit, and he went up the parade towards the bank and told nobody anything, quoted no figure, left no card and wrote nothing down.** Nobody was in that hall on that Saturday morning and the lamp died again a few minutes after the street light came up. **THE ONLY PERSON WHO FOUND OUT WAS THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP, WHO GOT AS FAR AS THAT DOOR AT ABOUT TEN PAST EIGHT THAT NIGHT AND STOPPED, AND WHO SAID THERE WAS A LIGHT ON AND SOMEBODY HAD PUT A LAMP IN THAT PORCH, AND THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES SAID AYE AND DID NOT PUT DOWN WHAT WAS IN HIS HAND. SHE SAID RIGHT, SAID SHE WAS not going to be the one to ask, AND SAID WHOEVER IT WAS HAD BETTER HAVE HAD A LADDER.** Nobody in that room said whether that lamp ought to be there and nobody in that town has said it to the man who put it in, and the man in the hi-vis was up that parade again on the Tuesday, for a job four doors along and did not go past that hall. **The man who works nights came up that parade on the Wednesday morning afterwards, saw a lit porch, and went past it. There is a bit of print under the switch giving the fitting's type and its wattage and an item number that nobody in this borough has had any reason to write down.**
- **A LAD IN FIVE ROOMS, AND ONE QUESTION FROM A SECOND PERSON.** On Friday the third of March a lad of about eighteen was in five rooms on one day, on two streets and in three buildings, and the first two were not the same room and not the same doorway. **There was one more chair in the shop that shuts early than there had been on the Tuesday** — the plain wooden one with the ply seat and the ring on the top of the back, standing by the wall and not in the row — **and the woman who runs that shop did not put no question to him about it and did not look at it twice, and he put it on the top of the stack laid as the others are and sitting at their angle, and she looked at the stack for a second and said two hundred years.** On a corridor in the building by the water the woman of about fifty-five told him there is a woman coming on Wednesday and would want a carrier bag with a folded blanket in it and something heavy under the blanket moved off that landing, and he carried it to the foot of that stair and did not open it and did not ask what was in it. **In the hall off the parade, the man in his forties — who had heard of him since the twenty-seventh of January, when the woman of about forty-two with the key said in front of nine people that there was a lad who does the washing-up at the parade and is in at about ten in the morning, and he said aye and went back to the pipe under the floor — set his cup down as the lad went by with the tray for the third occasion and asked him which of the two places he does first, and was told that this one is at ten and the Corrance one is at half past one, and said right and picked his cup up.** Eight seconds, and that was the whole of it. He went round that room four times with the tray, and nobody at that table said one word to him about his own life, and neither of the two words people use for somebody his age was used at that table that evening, and What the man from the parade has said since the summer stayed unsaid in that room. **On his way past the end of the table at about twenty to nine the lad heard the woman of about forty-two with the key tell the man in his forties that he had asked him something, and the man in his forties said it was a question about which of two places he does first, and she said aye and that he never asked her anything. That is the whole of the consequence, and the thing he asked in that room in June was not put to anybody in it again and was not answered by anybody in it.**
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot seven hundred and forty-nine days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post seven hundred and fifty-one days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 295–304 went near that room.** The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned.** **The drawer under the trade counter at the back of the shop is in no chapter of this batch either, was not opened, holds two sheets on top of each other, and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July, and THE WORD *DRAWER* IS AT ZERO IN ALL TEN CHAPTERS OF THIS BATCH, AS IS THE WORD *ENVELOPE*.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty and is not mentioned. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody was housed by anybody, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, the word *thank* is at zero in all ten, the word *eleven* is at zero in all ten, the word *drain* is at zero in all ten, the word *spring* is at zero in all ten, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** At the bottom of Peverell Street the pump is in the doorway and the stopcock is off, and the letterbox on number forty-one still has a leaflet in it from the summer, and **nobody in this borough has said one word about a hose to anybody.** The woman of about fifty-five is not housed by anybody in this batch: she is still in a room on a month, nobody put her in it and nobody arranged it, nobody arranged the door of her room and nobody asked her about it, and nobody has told her who put the hasp on.

**AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, now seventeen weeks old, with four wrong answers going round it instead of one, and it went round a bus shelter at the top of Marsh Lane in the open air on the fourteenth of February and the answer that came back was the first one, said again, and the woman who works in a shop said out loud on a pavement at that shelter that she had heard that one off him before, and the man who said it said it was the same answer and would not change for being said twice in two places, and a woman with a clipboard was spoken to for the first time in this account and said her course counts people and runs to the end of April.** The man who invented the first wrong answer is not in that room, is not told and must not be told. **From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a chair appeared in a shop and a lad carried it up with six others and nobody asked where it came from, and nine things were said aloud on a pavement and were not written down, and nobody counted either. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and the word *permission* is at zero in all ten chapters and nobody in these ten raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — one more piece of paper is named in this batch and not opened by anybody: a sheet of A4 with a hundred and twenty-four pounds on it and two boxes at the foot, which came under a door, was read twice by one man, went on a table beside a letting agent's card, and had a shop's name written in the first box and nothing in the second.****

**AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS, AND *NACRE* AND *RIVER STACKS* APPEAR NOWHERE IN CHAPTERS 295–304. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, THE SERIES OUTLINE SAYING A RIVER DISASTER AND MUTUAL-AID PERMISSIONS AND THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE PROMPT SAYING THE RECLAMATION SEASON AND EVERY OLD ROOT TERM EXPIRING IN A DIFFERENT PLACE, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND NO ROOT TERM EXPIRED AND NO RECLAMATION SEASON OPENED AND THE WORD *RECLAMATION* IN NO CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S: `outline/series.md` GIVES VOLUME 04 AS 145–192, VOLUME 05 AS 193–240, VOLUME 06 AS 241–288 AND VOLUME 07 AS 289–336, THE PAGE HAS 195 THROUGH 304 IN THE VOLUME 05 DIRECTORY, THE TWO DISAGREE AT BOTH ENDS, THERE IS NO FIGURE FOR THE END OF VOLUME 05 IN EITHER, THE FIGURE 242 IS USED BY NOBODY, NO CHAPTER IS MOVED BETWEEN DIRECTORIES, NO OUTLINE IS REWRITTEN, NO VOLUME 06 OUTLINE AND NO VOLUME-CLOSE PROMPT EXISTS, AND CARD 0304 IS NOT A CLOSE. AND THE SPELLING OF THE THIRD OF THE EIGHT PLACES, WHICH WAS NOT TOUCHED IN THIS BATCH BECAUSE THE EIGHT PLACES ARE NOT READ OUT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, SO NEITHER SPELLING APPEARS AND THE FLAG CARRIES FORWARD UNDECIDED; **THE CENSUS OF THE STATE FILES STANDS AT FIVE FILES PRINTING BOTH SPELLINGS, ONE PRINTING ONLY ONE AND ONE PRINTING ONLY THE OTHER, AND THAT CENSUS IS CARRIED AT §11 OF THE `state/open-threads.md` BLOCK AND IS NOT REPEATED HERE, BECAUSE THE 0010 PROMPT AS FIRST WRITTEN RESOLVED THAT FLAG IN ITS OWN FILE AND A FLAG RESOLVED IN ONE FILE AND OPEN IN ANOTHER IS TWO FLAGS AND A HUMAN HAS TO PICK.****

**AND TWO CARD COLLISIONS WERE FLAGGED IN CONTINUATION 0011 AND THE RULE WAS KEPT BOTH TIMES AND NEITHER CARD WAS DEFENDED. **CARD 0304 ASKED FOR *A BOROUGH, IN A SPRING*, WHICH TEN GAPS FROM TUESDAY 31 JANUARY 2017 CANNOT REACH ON EITHER ITS EARLIEST BOUND OF THURSDAY 2 MARCH 2017 OR ITS LATEST BOUND OF TUESDAY 11 APRIL 2017, AND 0304 TOOK THE DATE ITS OWN COUNT GAVE IT, WHICH IS FRIDAY 10 MARCH 2017, THE LATEST LEGAL DATE OF THE TEN, AND WAS GIVEN ORDINARY WEATHER INSTEAD, AND THE WORD *SPRING* IS AT ZERO IN ALL TEN CHAPTERS.** CARD 0298 PRINTED TWO FIGURES FOR ONE THING, A FOURTH COST IN ITS GOAL AND A THIRD COST IN ITS OWN MAJOR TURN, AND THE CHAPTER PRINTS A SHEET WITH A FIGURE ON IT AND DOES NOT NUMBER IT. **THE NEXT PHASE IS A CONTINUATION BATCH, CHAPTERS 305 ONWARD, DATED FROM SATURDAY 11 MARCH 2017, WHICH IS 0304'S OWN DATE PLUS ONE, AND IT IS NOT A VOLUME CLOSE.**


## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 314 — Monday the seventeenth of April 2017, from about eleven o'clock at night, in this city

**EIGHT HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-THREE DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, A JET NOZZLE HAS COME OFF A STANDPIPE IN A LANDING AND BEEN REPLACED WITH A CAP THAT WAS NOT THAT BUILDING'S, a wool blanket lies across the foot of a bed at the far end of a corridor, A SAUCEPAN WITH A CODE STAMPED INTO THE BASE OF IT IS ON A SHELF ABOVE TWO LIDS IT DOES NOT GO WITH, TWO SMALL IRON BED FRAMES WENT INTO TWO SKIPS FOUR DAYS APART, A STEP AT THE LOW END OF PEVERELL STREET IS STILL DOWN BECAUSE A MAN SAID OUT LOUD THAT HE WOULD TAKE IT UP, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN WENT THROUGH SIX DAYS IN FIVE ROOMS WITHOUT ONE QUESTION BEING PUT TO HIM, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS EIGHT HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-THREE DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** On Monday the seventeenth, at about half past eight, it went back onto a shelf in a room by the water. It had not been where it belongs since files were carried in off that corridor on the tenth of March and a binder propped against it by a woman unaware of what lay beneath; that Wednesday she carried the binder about two feet along to the next bay, the register following it an inch before stopping, and she squared the pair up. **The bay door does not catch and never has. THE LAST ENTRY IS A YARD LAMP, ITS BULB IN A COLUMN KEPT FOR MAINTENANCE, REPORTED OUT ABOUT NOVEMBER TWO YEARS AGO AND NOT NEAR SINCE, AND UNDER IT the blank rule below that last entry runs about two fingers and then stops, leaving some two inches of the page itself empty above the foot of a book of that depth. NOBODY IN THAT ROOM LOOKED AT ANY OF IT. TWO PEOPLE WERE IN THAT ROOM AND BOTH WERE IN A HURRY. on that corridor stands a door, and behind it a shelf, and on the shelf a bag tied round with string, twice over, AND THE BAG HAS NOT SHIFTED SINCE BEFORE THE WINTER.**
- **AND THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING.** Rain off the estuary from about half past seven that hangs in the air and soaks into a coat, and the boards on the parade through to wet by about nine and wet all day. The shop that shuts early went up at its own time, roughly ten to eight, much as it has since the middle of March, and a radio shop next door kept a shutter of its own chest-high, a man behind it and no volume on the set. **The man with the shovel was not down that parade until early afternoon and did no work at the bank end.** The gritting bin at the top of Peverell Street had a skin over what was left in its bottom, and one side punched clean through about the size of a closed hand. A white van turned into that street and stopped by the second house, a man got out and looked up the front of a building for about a minute in the rain, and it went back the way it had come, and nobody waved. The van with no sign on it was at the end of Marsh Lane on most days of any week with a tarpaulin bundled down over the top rail and nothing behind it. The step outside the shut shop is still down about two inches at the outside corner, its blue lino lying flat where the rain put it back, and the crack at the other end of it gone over with grit.
- **AN OBJECT IN AN ANGLE, AND THREE PEOPLE IT HAPPENED TO, NONE OF WHOM WAS HELPED BY IT.** On Saturday the eleventh of March a man in his thirties took a pine door off three hinges at a house in a terrace off the Cleeve road, where a new one goes in on the twenty-eighth, and it would not lie flat in the van, nor stand on end, the rear doors being shut and the front seat not folding, and stood it on its top edge in the angle where a back fence meets the end of a wall on a footpath at the low end of Peverell Street with the letter plate facing the fence, and told the woman who runs the shop behind that yard where it was going, and she said it was not their yard. **A woman with a pushchair came along that path, because the kerb is up at the front for two cars, and got through the remaining foot and a half one wheel at a time with the child against her hip, and the fence marked the top of the paintwork, and there was nobody there to speak to, and she came back the same way at about ten past two and it had gone on a hired van with four pieces of offcut under it. AT ABOUT FOUR IN THE AFTERNOON THE MAN IN HIS SIXTIES, WHO WAS IN A FIRST-FLOOR FRONT ROOM ON THE CORNER WITH THE CURTAINS OPEN AND A CUP HE HAD NOT DRUNK FROM AND WAS NOT DOING ANYTHING AT ALL, went out, came back, and switched on the lamp above that house's door, and nobody went under it that day or the next except her, AND SHE and she had no window to raise her eyes to. THE MAN IN HIS THIRTIES CAME BACK AT ABOUT TWO FOR HIS OWN VAN, SAW THE DOOR FROM SIX FOOT OFF, GOT IN AND WENT, AND DID NOT GO OVER AND LOOK AT THE FRONT OF IT. THE MAN WHO CAME FOR THE BOILER AT THAT SHOP TOOK THAT LANE INSTEAD OF THE PARADE BECAUSE A BARROW IS EASIER ON THE COBBLES, STOPPED IN THE MIDDLE OF IT, LOOKED AT A DOOR STANDING ON ITS TOP EDGE WITH A SCRAPE ALONG THE PAINTWORK AND NOTHING UNDER IT AND NOTHING TYING IT AND IT STILL STANDING AFTER WHATEVER WEATHER HAD COME IN BETWEEN, STOOD THERE FOR ABOUT TEN SECONDS, AND WENT BACK UP AND KNOCKED ON THE SIDE DOOR INSTEAD OF GOING IN THE FRONT. THE WOMAN WHO RUNS THAT shop has never learned that its boiler came round the back, nobody in that shop has ever learned of a door in that angle, and nobody went back to look at it.**
- **A SENTENCE THAT WENT INTO A KITCHEN, AND A FIGURE THAT CAME BACK WRONG BY A WORD.** On Wednesday the fifteenth of March, in the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, a man who repairs sewing machines and has been going to that hall most weeks for a good few years said, unasked, that a woman in the second house of Marsh Lane has been sent a bill that would put her landlord in a position, and why: a meter swapped in November, the old unit on a different supply, and two supplies billing one kitchen. The woman who works in a shop told him not to say that in there. The man in his forties told him to leave it and gave one reason, which is that a spoken thing leaves a room on the people in it and that woman cannot take hers back and did not put it there. **She took it out into the kitchen at about half past eight and said it there as well, and the young woman of about twenty said a figure of a hundred and ninety pounds, and nobody had given her that figure: it came from the woman who works in a shop, who had heard the post-office queue say over two hundred twice running the previous Friday and had said nothing to anybody for a fortnight. EACH OF THEM WORKED OUT WHOSE FIGURE IT WAS. THE HALL HEARD THAT THE CONVERSATION HAD GONE INTO A KITCHEN AND HEARD NO FIGURE AT ALL.** At the end of that room a folding table was said to be standing on two feet of scaffold board out of a skip on Cleeve Street in the summer, and nobody wrote it down and nobody took it off.
- **A CUPBOARD UNDER A STAIR, A BLANKET OUT, A BAR OF STEEL LEFT IN, AND A BAR THAT CANNOT REACH.** On Saturday the eighteenth a man in his thirties came into the building by the water at about ten past seven about a box on a first-floor landing and found the door of the cupboard under that stair standing open, and that it has no lock, and a key hanging inside it, on a hook, that has hung there since the building was finished, and inside a wool fire blanket in a bag, a foot of flat steel, cable ties, wire and a bucket. **The blanket went into his bag. THE STEEL WENT FOUR INCHES IN AND STOPPED. THE WOMAN IN THE ROOM NEXT DOOR ALONG, COUNTING PAST THE FIRE DOOR, WHOSE OWN DOOR STANDS OPEN THREE INCHES WITH A CHAIR AGAINST IT, TOLD HIM THAT A CUPBOARD WITH A BAR IN IT AND NO BLANKET IS A THING EVERY BUILDING IN THIS TOWN IS SHORT OF AND NOBODY HAS EVER SAID A WORD ABOUT, AND THAT A CUPBOARD WITH A BLANKET IN IT AND NOTHING ELSE IN IT IS A THING SOMEBODY COULD MAKE A NOISE ABOUT. HE PUT THE STEEL DOWN AND SHE SAID SHE WOULD NOT ASK HIM TO TAKE IT AND THEN ACT AS THOUGH SHE HAD NOT NOTICED, WOULD NOT CALL IT ALL RIGHT WHEN IT IS NOT A THING SHE CAN CALL ALL RIGHT TO, AND WOULD NOT PUT HER NAME TO A FOOT OF STEEL FOR THE REST OF HER LIFE.** He did not knock on the door of the woman of about fifty-five, and the reason given in the chapter is that a screwdriver, a bag of plaster and nine words in her dressing gown in February are the reason, and not the box. On the Monday morning at about half past eight the man in his forties found that the bar on that fire door comes out about four foot and stops, nine inches short of the edge of the cupboard door, and that the visiting round belongs to a firm from beyond the county boundary. He put a hand on the bar, saw it halt in mid-air, said right, and went downstairs. **The blanket is on the arm of that chair and it stays there for eight days. The key is still on its hook.**
- **THE THIRD THING THAT BUILDING DID, AND TWO OTHERS ON THE SAME WEEK.** On Sunday the twenty-sixth the woman of about fifty-five came down that passage with a jug because the stop tap in that building is off from Saturday night to Monday evening, and found the tap case standing open, the strip fitting lit, and the jet nozzle gone off the standpipe on the landing, with brass on the coupling where the plastic cap should be. **That brass is not the cap that came off the building, and this building's cap is a moulded plastic one on a chain that lives in the cupboard under the stairs with the blanket and the steel, such things being kept together in a building of that kind.** She stayed about a minute, closed the case and carried the water up. **THE PASSAGE HAD BEEN ENTERED BY SOMEBODY, AND NOBODY KNEW IT, NOBODY IN THIS BOROUGH KNOWS WHO CAPPED IT OR WHERE FOUR FOOT OF PIPE WENT, AND SHE DID NOT SAY IT TO A SOUL THAT DAY OR ON THE MONDAY AND HAS NOT SAID IT SINCE, AND SHE DID NOT PUT IT IN A LETTER.** At about two that afternoon the woman in the room next door along brought out a rolled wool blanket off the arm of that chair, knocked on the door at the end of the corridor, and gave her reasons — a good blanket, no mark on it, a bit too big for the bed she has, and a radiator off since before Christmas — having seen a tea towel and a cardigan from where she stood. She did not go in, was not asked, and did not say where it came from. The woman of about fifty-five received it in both arms, did not open it, and laid it over the end of the bed. **ON THE MONDAY MORNING AT ABOUT TEN TO EIGHT THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES LET HIMSELF IN AT THAT STREET DOOR WITH MORTAR MIX AND A TROWEL AND BEDDED IN THE LOOSE FRONT LEFT CORNER OF THE STEP OUTSIDE THAT DOOR, WHICH HAD BEEN MOVING UNDERFOOT SINCE BEFORE CHRISTMAS AND MAKING A NOISE LIKE SOMEBODY KNOCKING TWICE, AND HE DID IT BEFORE THE FIRST PERSON DOWN THAT PASSAGE HAD THE TAP OPEN AND TOLD NOBODY IN THIS BOROUGH THAT HE HAD DONE IT.** A brass cap is still on that outlet, a wool blanket is still on that bed, and the two are not connected by anybody. The three bottles of washing-up that were standing on that first-floor landing in the middle of the week are on a shelf in a bin store at the foot of that stair. **The woman of about fifty-five is still in that room on a month. A door in that building caught her across the forearm at twenty past two on the third of February. Nobody arranged the door of her room, nobody asked her about it, nobody told her who fitted the hasp on the inside of it, and nobody in that building has ever spoken to her about any part of that door.**
- **ONE BILL DESCRIBED TWICE, AT TWO FIGURES, BY TWO PEOPLE WHO HAD NEVER MET.** On Thursday the twenty-third of March the man who sells radios, whose shutter on that parade is at half height most days, told the woman with a shopping bag that a bill in the second house of Marsh Lane is over two hundred, has been twice running, is not a misread, and that the question to put to a landlord is which meter he read, on the ground that a landlord claiming to have read both has read a unit that is not there. **A man of about thirty at the shelter at the bottom of that parade had been given the same bill described at a hundred and ninety pounds, in February, by a man he worked with who has gone to work thirty miles off, and he kept it because it was said twice the same way, and he is a man who reads figures off the bottom of things and was not going to invent one to be even with anybody.** They met at the shelter and did not settle it: she said one of them is talking about a different house or a different quarter or one of them is wrong, and that she was not standing at a bus stop working it out; he said he was not going to pretend he had not got it. There is no paper on any of it and nobody has anything to show anybody. Later that evening the man who sells radios put it again to a man buying a battery, who never met her and was gone inside four minutes. On the Saturday the woman with a shopping bag went past that shop and neither of them said a word. **he is unaware that anybody in this borough is carrying a figure for her kitchen at all.**
- **TWO SMALL IRON BED FRAMES, FOUR WEEKS AND THREE DOORS APART, BOTH IN SKIPS.** On Thursday the thirtieth of March the man in his thirties put one against the gable wall in the yard behind the shop that shuts early, head end up, foot end about three foot out into the yard, having carried it on the boards of the van since about the middle of the month out of a back bedroom on a road off the Cleeve one, and told the woman who runs that shop where it was going, and she said it was not their yard. **at that counter four days a week stands the woman who works in a shop WITH A WIRED GLASS DOOR BEHIND IT AND LOOKED AT THAT FRAME ABOUT FOUR TIMES A DAY FOR A FORTNIGHT AND DID NOT GO OUT INTO THAT YARD ONCE, AND DID NOT ASK WHERE IT CAME FROM, and in the fortnight he walked that parade perhaps nine times and never used that gate and never reversed the frame. It went into the skip at about twenty to six that Monday, with pram things, a bicycle wheel and a television, and the bin man observed that he had not known such a thing could go in a skip.** A man of about twenty-eight with a unit off that parade had had one of his own since about the middle of January and had been sitting on it, with a folded blanket on the seat and two milk crates in front, and four weeks later on a Sunday he carried his out to the back of a shop three doors along because his unit's roof drips onto the top rail of it, and stood it on its side against brick and cleared the wet leaves at the low end with a length of stick. Nobody saw him. On the Monday the landlord's men took that yard with a skip. He went in on the Tuesday, put his coat on, looked at the wall, and has said nothing to anybody about it since. **from the parade, through a gate on its latch, the second frame was seen by the woman who works in a shop, STOOD ON THE PAVEMENT LONG ENOUGH TO TAKE IN WHAT IT WAS, THE FRAME BEHIND HER OWN SHOP NOT ENTERING HER HEAD, AND WENT ON TO THE BUS STOP. THE PERSON WHO LEFT THE FIRST ONE WAS NEVER ASKED ABOUT IT BY ANYBODY.**
- **A STEP HE SAID HE WOULD TAKE UP, TWICE, AND DID NOT.** On Wednesday the fifth of April a man in his thirties knelt on the step outside the a shop at that street's low end, shut since the summer before last, put a new plug in each of the old holes, found it to be a lip and not a hole, and said out loud that he would take it up, screed it, and have it done inside a fortnight, and a man in a fleece going to the works said aye and drank his coffee and said that a screed in that step would be indistinguishable from its neighbours in ten years and the man responsible would not be him. At about ten past eight the next morning he said the same thing again in the same words and louder, to a different man at the open end of that hard, the woman keeping her count nearby and facing away, and wrote one line in pencil on the top sheet of a pad of work notes he had not opened since the summer. **He did not do it that week or the week after, and by the middle of April that lino had settled onto the tarmac of its own accord and lay flat enough for nobody to catch a toe on it, AND THE STEP IS STILL TWO INCHES DOWN AND WILL GO ON BEING TWO INCHES DOWN.** His reason in the chapter is that he can screed a step and charge for it, that there is no freeholder of it in this borough to invoice, and that a man who raises a step for a shop he has no trade with becomes, to that shop, a stranger who has been near it. On the Thursday evening in the hall off the parade the woman with a clipboard said that on the Tuesday, in the hour and a half before the rain, she counted four hundred and something, and that she had heard a man in his thirties at the top of that lane say he would take a step up in a voice you could hear over a bus. He came in at about ten to nine and said he never said he would do that step, and said he had said he would have a look at it, and that a look costs a boot and about two minutes while the work costs a whole Sunday that does not come back. **She said she does not need to be right and only needs to have heard it. The man in a fleece said that is what he heard and that is what he told the woman with the board, and that he could not say what the man in his thirties was doing at that lane because their hours differ.** He laid the pad on that table face up for about a second, one pencil line on the top sheet and nothing else on it, and pocketed it again, and said nobody had asked him to do a step and there is nothing in it. **A LIST IS NOT A DOCUMENT, A LIST IS NOT A FORM, THERE IS NO FORM IN THIS BOROUGH, A THING SAID OUT LOUD IS NOT A THING ANYBODY KEEPS, AND ON THE MONDAY AFTER, she handed the day's figures in at the back of a bank and kept nothing of her own.**
- **A PAN WITH A CODE STAMPED INTO THE BASE OF IT.** On Saturday the eighth of April it was standing in a back yard beside an open skip on a lorry, on its side with about nine inches of frozen water in the bottom, with no lid near it and none in the house it came out of either. The base carries a letter, six figures and a date, and the date reads twenty sixteen, and the six figures are nobody's name, not a postcode, and not a number anybody here has a use for, and there is no maker's name on the object at all. The man with the van put the water out, turned it over, read the base, and came up a parade at about half past five and into the hall off it, because one thing is known about the man who works nights and that is that he once stood in a coal line at the end of Marsh Lane and took nothing out of it. **He was let in and stood by the counter about four minutes with it in his hand, offered no drink, and asked for none, and he asked who the man was and was told none of them and that he comes in about half past ten. He said it was for him, that the code is no use to anybody, and that the year on the base is the only thing about it worth knowing, and put it on the end of the table by the door and went out into the rain, and nobody in that room mentioned a saucepan.** The woman who works in a shop did not put it in the cupboard with the urn and did not take it to the kitchen. The night man arrived at about ten past ten, still in his coat, found it at the door end, noted there was no name on it, was told he never gave the man his name, turned it over, read the stamp without saying it out loud, and said aye, well. **He heated about a third of a tin of something in it on the cooker at the rear of that parade's kitchen at about ten to midnight, ate it standing up, washed it out, dried it on the cloth, and put it at the back of the shelf above the lids, behind two lids that go with nothing, because what is not his does not lie about a table in a full room. He said nothing to that room about having warmed anything in it, and on the Wednesday the woman who works in a shop went in, found nothing there, and left the question alone, and by Thursday the man with the van was at the end of Marsh Lane, told nothing about a pan in a cupboard in A KITCHEN.**
- **SIX DAYS, FIVE ROOMS, AND NOT ONE QUESTION.** On Thursday the thirteenth of April a lad of about eighteen was in a kitchen at the back of a parade, the floor of the shop that shuts early on that parade, a kitchen in Corrance Lane, a corridor on the first floor of a building by the water and a hall off that parade, on six days and not one, and nobody put a single question to him in any of them. The sixth day is there his employer's kitchen having no employer from the Monday, and the woman not wanting him back in there at the end of it. **he halted mid-shop with a chair in each hand, waiting to hear where the eighth one went, was given three words, was answered two hundred years, and was told there might be more on the Saturday if the weather held. A MAN AT THE END OF THAT COUNTER HAD A PAPER AND A PEN ALL MORNING and got not one word out of him between the opening and the close. ON HIS THIRD PASS IN THAT HALL HE STOPPED DELIBERATELY IN A GAP BESIDE THE TABLE WITH A TRAY IN HIS HANDS, AND THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES LOOKED UP AND THEN LOOKED BACK DOWN AT HIS CUP, AND NOTHING CAME. HE CARRIED THREE BOTTLES OF WASHING-UP FROM A FIRST-FLOOR LANDING DOWN TO A BIN STORE ONE AT A TIME BECAUSE HIS BUCKET WAS GOING TO GO OVER THE BOTTOM OF THEM, AND THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE CAME OUT WHILE HE WAS FINISHING AND SAID SHE WOULD BE IN.** At about ten past three, on that corridor with a mop, he and told the woman in the room next door along that there had been a fire blanket in the cupboard under the stairs in a bag, and that there is a bar of iron under it, and that there is a key hanging on a hook inside that door, and that a man had been and taken the blanket out, and that he saw it on the chair outside her door on the Saturday and on the Monday it was not on the chair, and that you do not have to lock it and nobody does. **SHE STOOD AGAINST HER OWN FRAME AND SAID NOTHING AT ALL, and then turned her eyes to the far wall, AND HE WENT DOWN THE STAIR AND OUT. THE BLANKET IS ON THE END OF A BED AT THE END OF THAT CORRIDOR, and to say anything at all she would have to name where it went, and she will not.** He went round that hall four times with a tray and every hand at that table reached for that tray and took a cup, the only talk of that evening was the meter in that porch wanting seeing to before the summer, the woman with the key raising it and the man in his forties agreeing, and that was the whole of that evening's talk. **The bus up the parade was half full and he boarded it at the near end and climbed to the upper deck, and a man by the front with a folded paper, and the lad said the step outside the shop that is shut is still down and is nobody's to be doing and that he has not done it and is not saying he is going to, and the man identified it as the one with the lino on it, said aye, and went back to his paper, and he got off at the top of the parade and walked.** He is on the dishes, he has no title, grade, department, chair, seat, membership, form or row on a rota, being asked is not a standing, being useful is not a job and being in a room is not being a member, and the question he asked on the fourth of June 2016 was not put to anybody in that room again and was not answered by anybody in it.
- **FOUR ANSWERS ARE STILL GOING ROUND THAT TABLE AND NONE OF THEM CAN BE PUT BESIDE ANY OTHER.** Since the twelfth of January there have been four of them on that table, and those still there are the ones that were there in January, and no one has ever managed to set any one of the four beside any of the other three, and nobody at that table has been able to say what the four amount to together. The woman who works in a shop said nobody has put a fifth one on and nobody has taken one of the four off, and that nobody has said where any of them came from, and that the one concerning a street concerns the same street it did in January, and the man in his forties agreed with that and left it there. **She also said that a man had put the first of those four out loud at a shelter on that lane in February's wind, and that the woman keeping the count answered that she had heard it from him first, and that nothing of a fifth kind has been produced by anybody since, including nobody at that table.** The man from the parade put his question again that Monday, to the table and to nobody, in words that are not the words he used in 0299, and 0299 is the chapter that has him say it, and where the two differ the chapter governs. The man in his sixties said something was happening at the bank with the wet on that lamp, and the table let it lie. **THE MAN WHO INVENTED THE FIRST OF THOSE FOUR ANSWERS IS NOT IN THAT ROOM AND IS NOT TOLD AND IS NOT GOING TO BE.** That gas ran out at about ten past ten and the padlock went on the case, the chairs went up, the woman of about fifty-five was among the last out, and the porch lamp was lit as the door opened and went out some fifteen minutes afterwards.
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot seven hundred and eighty-seven days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post seven hundred and eighty-nine days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 305–314 went near that room.** The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned. **The drawer under the trade counter at the back of the shop is in no chapter of this batch either, was not opened, holds two sheets on top of each other, and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July, and **THE WORD *DRAWER* IS AT ZERO IN ALL TEN CHAPTERS OF THIS BATCH, AS IS THE WORD *ENVELOPE*.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty and is not mentioned. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody was housed by anybody, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, the word *thank* is at zero in all ten, the word *eleven* is at zero in all ten with the datelines removed, the word *drain* is at zero in all ten, the word *spring* is at zero in all ten, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** The order is not in force and was not brought back to life by anything in this batch. The empty square is still empty. The boiler on Carrow Lane is still in a chapel that has not been lit. The handrail is still on four outside steps. The bath is still on its side in a yard under a gable end. The lamp in that porch is still in it, on a dusk sensor. The chest of hinges is still in a shed behind a shop. The locker with the hundred-and-fifty-five-pound sheet on it still has the hundred-and-twenty-four-pound sheet beside it and a shop's name in the first box and nothing in the second. The man who works nights saw that lit porch and went past it. The man at number forty-one is not knocked on. Jonas Mercer is in no chapter of this batch and is asked nothing.

**AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, and the thing has gone one place further: it was said in a Methodist hall on Carrow Lane about a water bill, on a subject that is not the register at all, and the woman who works in a shop told the man who said it to leave it, the man in his forties told him to leave it and said why, and she took it out into the kitchen beside that hall and said it there as well, and the hall heard that it had gone into a kitchen and heard no figure at all. No fifth of anything has been produced and the twenty-eighth of June has not been put back to four. Separately, one water bill has been described at two different figures on the same parade inside one week by two people who had never met, and neither was wrong on purpose, and nobody put them together.** From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a lid came off a cupboard under a stair and was not locked and nobody asked the woman who owns that cupboard, a fire blanket went on a bed, a pad came out of a pocket and went on a table for a second, and nobody was asked and nobody asked anybody. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and the word *permission* is at zero in all ten chapters and nobody in these ten raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — no piece of paper was created in this batch; the things that came were a code stamped into the base of a saucepan and one line in pencil on the top sheet of a pad with an elastic round it, which is on nothing and which nobody has a copy of.

**AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS, AND *NACRE* AND *RIVER STACKS* APPEAR NOWHERE IN CHAPTERS 305–314. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, THE SERIES OUTLINE SAYING A RIVER DISASTER AND MUTUAL-AID PERMISSIONS AND THE VOLUME 04 CLOSE PROMPT SAYING THE RECLAMATION SEASON AND EVERY OLD ROOT TERM EXPIRING IN A DIFFERENT PLACE, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND NO ROOT TERM EXPIRED AND NO RECLAMATION SEASON OPENED AND THE WORD *RECLAMATION* IN NO CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S: `outline/series.md` GIVES VOLUME 04 AS 145–192, VOLUME 05 AS 193–240, VOLUME 06 AS 241–288 AND VOLUME 07 AS 289–336, THE PAGE HAS 195 THROUGH 314 IN THE VOLUME 05 DIRECTORY, THE TWO DISAGREE AT BOTH ENDS, THERE IS NO FIGURE FOR THE END OF VOLUME 05 IN EITHER, THE FIGURE 242 IS USED BY NOBODY, NO CHAPTER IS MOVED BETWEEN DIRECTORIES, NO OUTLINE IS REWRITTEN, NO VOLUME 06 OUTLINE AND NO VOLUME-CLOSE PROMPT EXISTS, AND CARD 0314 IS NOT A CLOSE. AND THE SPELLING OF THE THIRD OF THE EIGHT PLACES, WHICH WAS NOT TOUCHED IN THIS BATCH BECAUSE THE EIGHT PLACES ARE NOT READ OUT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, SO NEITHER SPELLING APPEARS AND THE FLAG CARRIES FORWARD UNDECIDED; **THE CENSUS OF THE STATE FILES STANDS AT FIVE FILES PRINTING BOTH SPELLINGS, ONE PRINTING ONLY ONE AND ONE PRINTING ONLY THE OTHER, AND THAT CENSUS IS CARRIED AT §11 OF THE `state/open-threads.md` BLOCK AND IS NOT REPEATED HERE, BECAUSE THE 0010 PROMPT AS FIRST WRITTEN RESOLVED THAT FLAG IN ITS OWN FILE AND A FLAG RESOLVED IN ONE FILE AND OPEN IN ANOTHER IS TWO FLAGS AND A HUMAN HAS TO PICK.****

**AND TWO FLAGS WERE TAKEN IN CONTINUATION 0012 AND BOTH RULES WERE KEPT. **CARD 0314 ASKED FOR *A BOROUGH, IN A MONTH*, WHICH ITS OWN COUNT COULD GIVE IT, AND THE RULE THAT BINDS WAS APPLIED ANYWAY: THE WORD *SPRING* IS AT ZERO IN ALL TEN CHAPTERS, NO CHAPTER CALLS ITS OWN MONTH A TURN OR A CHANGE OF FORTNIGHT OR A CHANGE OF ANYTHING, AND 0314 WAS GIVEN ORDINARY WEATHER INSTEAD, WHICH IS RAIN.** AND THE PROMPT'S OWN ARITHMETIC AT THE SHORT END IS INTERNALLY INCONSISTENT: IT COUNTS TEN GAPS AND THEN MEASURES THE SHORT BOUND AS THE FLOOR PLUS THIRTY DAYS, WHICH TREATS THE INCOMING ONE-DAY FLOOR GAP AS A THREE-DAY GAP; ON STRICT ARITHMETIC THE SHORTEST LEGAL TENTH CHAPTER IS SATURDAY 8 APRIL 2017. A FLOOR THAT IS TOO HIGH IS A PERMISSIVE ERROR RATHER THAN A MERE TYPO BECAUSE IT LETS A WRITER COMPRESS BELOW THE THREE-DAY MINIMUM, SO THE LATER AND SAFER PAIR WAS KEPT AND 0314 WAS PLACED AT MONDAY THE SEVENTEENTH OF APRIL 2017, WHICH SATISFIES BOTH PAIRS. NOTHING WAS COMPRESSED AND NO CARD WAS DEFENDED. **THE NEXT PHASE IS A CONTINUATION BATCH, CHAPTERS 315 ONWARD, DATED FROM TUESDAY THE EIGHTEENTH OF APRIL 2017, WHICH IS 0314's OWN DATE PLUS ONE, AND IT IS NOT A VOLUME CLOSE.**

## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 324 — Saturday the twenty-seventh of May 2017, from about nine in the evening, in this city

**NINE HUNDRED AND THIRTEEN DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, A BUILDING WENT THREE DAYS WITH NO WATER IN IT BECAUSE SOMEBODY PUT THE TAP BACK ON AT THE WRONG END OF A WEEKEND, AN UPRIGHT VACUUM CLEANER IS LYING OVER ON ITS SIDE IN A CORNER BY THE FIRE DOOR ON A LANDING IN THAT BUILDING, TWO FOLDED METAL FRAMES ARE AGAINST BRICK IN TWO YARDS A LANE APART A MONTH APART, TWO SLATES ARE ON A GABLE WITH THEIR NAILS STANDING HALF AN INCH PROUD, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN WENT THROUGH ONE DAY IN FIVE ROOMS WITHOUT ONE TASK BEING PUT TO HIM AND SAID ONE THING TO A WOMAN'S BACK IN A CORRIDOR, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS NINE HUNDRED AND THIRTEEN DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** It stands on the second shelf up in a bay off a first-floor corridor in the building by the water, on a door that does not shut over the catch. The final entry is a yard lamp, entered as gone about November two years ago under a heading reserved for that sort of thing, and nothing has been written under that reserved heading since. Below that line the paper runs on a hand's width and then gives up about two inches short of the book's foot, and nobody left that gap; the book has it. **Two women came into that room mid-morning for a chair out of a corridor being painted, carried the one at the end of it out between them, passed more than six foot from that bay, and did not know what was on the shelf. The door stood open behind them when they went.**
- **AND THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING.** First dry morning since before Christmas, and about nine birds lined along a gutter in a row facing one way, over the roof tops inside four minutes and not seen in that street again. A greengrocer's shutter up with a man on a step inside it. The shop that shuts early down since the Thursday afternoon. Two sets on a pavement board with marker prices on small cards gone soft. A hand of gravel and a dry twig in the gritting bin, and the hole in one side of it still about the size of a closed hand. A unlettered white van, which is there almost always and never on a rota.
- **A PIPE OFF A HOUSE ON THE TUESDAY, AND A FOOT OF SCAFFOLD PUT UNDER IT UNASKED.** A man in a fleece is on about nine houses a morning and is on nobody's list. His van's offside mirror took a downpipe, two brackets and two foot of pipe off a rendered return, and the plaster behind the top bracket had been wet behind the paint for years. The man in his thirties could not do that house — brick where he would want timber, and the corner wants a tower already booked for the week after — so he laid the pipe in the channel and went on round. Coming past at a quarter past twelve he spent five minutes on it without being asked: a length of scaffold split, stood on end where the render returns, the pipe leaned onto it, wire round all three. **He said before and after that it is a prop and not a repair, and that the wall wants opening up in the autumn. The other man finished the round about ten past six and nobody telephoned him about any of it. The woman who works in a shop had it from the woman who runs that shop, is not going out to ask him about it, and will sell him a roll of paper like anybody else. He came in on the Saturday, paid, left, and has not been back, and is unaware that either of those two women spoke about him.**
- **A SENTENCE ABOUT A BANK LAMP, AND A SECOND ROOM HEARING IT.** On the twenty-second of April, in the hall, the man in his sixties reported a lamp over a bank door burning in broad daylight on the twentieth with two people under it working out how high it hung before going in, and reported it off every time since, and said himself he ought to have had it somewhere else. The woman who works in a shop reduced it to two rooms and neither of them being anywhere any of them work, and put two questions to the man in his thirties about how long the lamp had been burning and whose it was, and got neither answered. The man who works nights asked whether it belonged with the four and was told it does not and that nobody is setting it beside them. **The man in his forties then told her, in front of the man in his thirties, that it has already been said in another room in this town, so it is in five places and nobody is going to any of them. No fifth answer was produced, none of the four was corrected or added to, and the twenty-eighth of June was not mentioned.**
- **A LENGTH OF FOUR INCH WITH NO SECOND PERSON ON IT.** On the twenty-seventh, off Cleeve Street, a drainage firm capped nothing, so the man in his thirties cut the old stack off, got a new length up through a landing ceiling on a cord, and stood it on the step with its top end about seven foot in the air. A coupling cannot be run onto a length in the air at the end of itself and the job wanted two people. **The woman who works in a shop lives in the top flat, was the only person in that building that morning, and would not stand under it while somebody worked a caulk iron in the joint.** Her side: a hole in her ceiling, a hopper going down four floors onto open pipe, a blockage in about six weeks, and a man who comes two days a month. His side: the same six weeks, but a stranger's fault on a step, and nobody's name on any paper because there is nothing to put it on. A man with a folded trolley and a set of steps took a fridge out of the ground-floor flat in about four minutes, was asked nothing and offered nothing, and afterwards the man in his thirties observed that a man halfway up a stair with a fridge on it is not an arm anybody can borrow. **Left on that landing: a capped stack, a five-inch hole with old plaster round it, and that job's tools and offcuts still against the wall.**
- **A CAFE DESCRIBED TWICE THE SAME MORNING WITH THE SUBJECTS THE WRONG WAY ROUND.** On the thirtieth, at an open stop at the end of Marsh Lane, the man in a fleece told a man with a dog about a cafe, a man from the city, both shops, and the radio man's August, giving his own standing as the fourth remove of it. About twenty minutes later, with that shop's shutter already hooked up, the man who puts shutters up told a young man with a rucksack the better-sourced version: the woman who runs that shop has bought the radio shop, the radio man is out by the end of June, and the two are to be done as one parade, two removes off a fitter's brother-in-law. **Then he said the thing that matters: both shutters came off the same bar in the same week, so whoever buys buys a parcel, and a man who sells radios is not the man who ends up running a cafe — so one of the two men has it backwards and it is not the one whose version is more interesting.** Nobody went in, nobody knocked, nobody telephoned, no paper arrived, the shop went up on the Monday at its own time and shut at two.
- **A FOURTH THING THAT BUILDING DID, AND A STAIR TREAD.** That building's stop tap sits behind a board inside a case at the bottom of the passage and stays closed from the last evening of a week until one of them opens it. This time somebody put it back the wrong side of the weekend and nobody came back for it, so there was no water in the building for three days. The tap was found across, its board lifted out, its case standing open, and nobody can account for that. Three mornings, two empty jugs each, a cold tap giving about two seconds and stopping. **The meter on that passage wall ran for all three days and is the one thing in the building that got what it wanted, and nobody living there has ever taken a reading off it.** In the middle of those days the woman in the room next door along saw the two jugs and an unplugged kettle through a door she leaves open a hand's width, and offered half a bucket of her own washing-up water for the kettle and for washing, on terms that nothing be said and nobody fetch it, **and was refused**, and has not been out on that landing since and has told nobody. Water came back on the Tuesday morning; she filled both jugs and never found out who put the tap across, having heard somebody on the landing about half past ten that night and not opened her door. **The other half: the second tread up that building's outside stair lifts half an inch and knocks once, and on the Thursday the man in his forties took its front corner up, found one screw sheared and one pulled out with a plug the size of a finger, drilled the hole out, drove a longer screw through the bracket into sound concrete and put his weight on it twice. It did not move and made no noise. He went back down without knocking on a single door, and a few feet below him on the stair the woman of about fifty-five went up past him with both jugs full and a kettle hooked over one arm, and not one word passed between them.**
- **TWO FOLDED METAL GREENHOUSE FRAMES, A MONTH APART, IN TWO YARDS ON ONE LANE.** A frame in three sections, six foot by two foot out, folding flat, painted steel, the weight of a man, with a bent corner on the middle one. In the first week of April a woman leaned one against the brick of a yard at the top of that lane, having got it there on a barrow in three goes, and **nobody asked her a single question about it in any direction, and she has not gone up that lane again since**, and it has stood five weeks with green along the foot where that edge stands in a puddle that never drains off that side. On the tenth of May the man in his thirties propped another against brick in a yard two doors above a shop and would not put it on a roof rack because of the wind coming up off the back of the parade. He told the woman who works in a shop, from the alley, that it belongs to nobody, will be there a few days and then go, and that the honest answer is that he knows of four in this town: two up a garden, one a bench in a garage, one against a wall further up the same lane. **She did not go and look. He had forgotten the first one. No single person in this borough saw both, and the woman who put the first one down has not been asked about it by anybody, then or since.**
- **TWO SLATES ON A GABLE, A FORTNIGHT SAID ON A PAVEMENT, AND A WOMAN WHO ASKED FOR IT IN WRITING.** That corner house is two storey, with a bay at the bottom and a gable above it carrying about fifty slates; the two lowest on the left have slipped about two inches and are still on rusted nails with the heads standing half an inch proud, and one is the size of a door, and a woman sits in that bay with a table and a plant on it most days. **On the morning of the fifth of May the man in his thirties told a man in a fleece what the job wants — a tower, somebody to build it, somebody else to hold the boards, a day and a half of a weekend, his own yard to get the tower out of — and said in the same breath that nobody in that house had asked him, and that he had never spoken to the woman and never intended to.** In a shop off the Cleeve road a fortnight later he got nothing said about it at all, and the man in a fleece looked at the floor of the shop instead of at him. Ten days on, the woman in the bay stopped him in the road, having got his name from the man in a fleece, who had been at her door on the Thursday and had said he did not know the man's name. She put it to him that she had been told he said a fortnight. He does not deny the fortnight and denies only that he ever said he would do the job. **She asks for a piece of paper, not the work: a name and a date kept in her front room, so that if a slate comes off in the autumn she can say she asked a man in that road and he wrote it down and did not come. He refuses, on two grounds — there is nothing in this town to put his name on, and a paper with his name at the top would be the only one in the borough and would be about two slates; and if her street went into his pad, then a slate through that window would put him in it, whereas written nothing he is not in it.** He tells her what is over her head and that he will not tell her twice. She goes in and leaves the sash down. **The man in a fleece has said nothing about any of it, and there is nothing written down by the man in his thirties either.**
- **AN UPRIGHT VACUUM CLEANER TAKEN IN, USED ONCE, PUT IN A CORNER.** A good upright with a fifteen-foot lead and a cracked cuff at the machine end, on the boards of the man with the van since the second week of April, out of a house on the Cleeve road emptied for a woman now living a long way off, out of a cupboard under that house's stairs, the filter holding somebody's hair, taken because the woman from the letting agency said take it with the loader running. **The whole of what that man knows about the woman of about fifty-five is that in the third week of January he was in the yard behind that building late on a weekday afternoon with a torch in his hand on a pipe and a light was burning in a first-floor front room in broad daylight, and he has thought about that light since.** The woman who works in a shop told him there is one person on that floor, that she is not going up with him and is not telling him anything about her, and that she will open the street door and take an end of it because that stair gives a person barely three feet of width from the bottom to the top and she is not having a woman of fifty-five do that alone — and says that the stair is her only reason. She told him to leave it on the landing and not bring it into her room, which is about nine foot each way and already holds a bed, a chair and a table. **He set it against that wall, came back down, she went up and shut her own door, he raised a hand from the cab two days later and she raised one, and that was the whole of it, and he has not gone up that street again, and nobody in this borough has ever told him what became of the machine.** She ran it once on a Sunday over about twenty feet of concrete, brush off and the tube on the cracked cuff, because five weeks of wind had got sand into the junctions, along the fire door and into a threshold, and a broom reaches none of it. Fifteen foot of lead against a landing of twenty-six, so it was done in two goes and the plug moved, and the filter came up with a bag of that landing's own grit in it, and the noise brought the woman in the room next door along out of her door; she said aye, well, and went back in. **It went over on its side in the corner where that landing widens at the fire door, its lead on top of it, against a wall that is not straight, and it has not been run again and the filter has not been emptied.**
- **ONE DAY, FIVE ROOMS, AND NOT ONE TASK PUT TO THAT LAD.** On the twenty-second of May he was in five places in the order the dateline gives them, on one day and not six, and not one person put a task to him in any of them. The first was the kitchen at the back of a parade, and it was clean before he got there — surfaces wiped and dry, both hobs off, the rack empty, the board empty, no third glass anywhere in it — and the man who cooks there had left about nine the previous night and left no note. **At the greengrocer's a delivery of six boxes and a sack went down in the middle of the shop and he was not asked to move one of them; the woman put one sack on her own shoulder and shoved the other two into the cold room with her foot, and wished him a good morning at about ten past twelve.** In the house at the end of Marsh Lane the man was out, the woman was not back until about eight, nobody knew he was in the house, and he sat at that table for half an hour without going upstairs. In the corridor there was nothing to mop and he went along it with the bucket anyway, then stood at the top of the outside stair about ten minutes. **Just before midday the woman of about fifty-five came out of the room at the far end with two empty jugs, went down, and shut the street door at the bottom of that outside stair, and was back up that flight about twenty-five minutes later with both full and a kettle hooked over the arm that had no jug on it. He said, not loudly and to her back while she was going through her own door, that he is in that building about eight on a Monday and she is on that stair at about half past seven with the two empty ones, and that he has seen her and she has not seen him, and that he was not going to get her to swing round on a landing with a jug in each hand to hear it. She said nothing and the door shut, and the woman next door along, whose own door never shuts further than a hand's width, said nothing either.** In the hall he is not on the tea any more and has not been since before Christmas and nobody in it has said so and he has not asked; the urn was already on, two cups were drying on a cloth, the table was set for four and he was not one of the four, and nobody said one word to him, and the man in his thirties put his usual thing on the table and nobody in that room took any notice of it in front of the lad. **He left at a quarter to seven and walked the two miles, having gone back for his own mop and bucket at half past four and left the pair of them in a bin store at the head of that parade, and no one in this borough knows what that walk costs him in time.**
- **AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS WHERE IT WAS.** A card has been in a slot eight hundred and twenty-seven days and has not been taken out, put up or read, and it is in no chapter of this batch. A letter has been in a tray on the second floor under two other pieces of post eight hundred and twenty-nine days, sealed, and **no chapter of chapters 315–324 went near that room.** The page of 29 May 2015 is not in force and its one reader has not been asked again. The nine printed sheets of 19 June still read *no requirement stated* and have no name box. The column headed *date seen again* is still empty. The counter in the vestibule is in no scene in this batch, answered nothing and was not entered, and **the drawer under it is not opened, not counted, not asked about and not mentioned, and the word *drawer* is at zero in all ten chapters, as is the word *envelope*.** The rota is not rebuilt and the 11:00–13:30 row is still empty and is not mentioned. The second line under Bram Osei's Thursday has not been looked at. The board is at seven of nine. The ninth seat is fifty-four and unpaid and untold. The buff file of 14 October 2011 is not opened and four people have seen the bay number and the date on that cover. Paragraph 4.11 is not answered and the compliance week is not verified. Roy Albery does not walk again. **Nobody died, nobody was rescued, nobody was housed by anybody, nobody renewed, expired or renamed anything, nothing was minuted, no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind, the word *thank* is at zero in all ten, the word *eleven* is at zero in all ten with the datelines removed, the word *drain* is at zero in all ten, the word *spring* is at zero in all ten, and the eleven standing refusals are eleven.** The order is not in force and was not brought back to life.

- **AND THE SIX OPEN QUESTIONS ARE ALL STILL OPEN AND NONE WAS ANSWERED. From 0184, which dated thing will be the first somebody can prove — **still open, and the thing has gone one place further in the other direction: it is in five rooms and nobody is going to any of them, it was said in a hall on Carrow Lane about a lamp over a bank door, it was carried into the room at the back of a shop by a man who did not know he was carrying it, and the man in his sixties was told in that second room that he has already said it in a fifth room he would not name. A man asked a question about that lamp and was asked two questions back and answered neither. No fifth of anything has been produced and the twenty-eighth of June was not mentioned, and on the Saturday the man from the parade put a different question on that table and the whole room agreed out loud that it stays a question.** From 0174, which of the eight will be the first somebody decides to keep — untouched in this batch. From 0164, whether the page or the chair is the first thing a person is asked to agree to — a machine was brought to a door and taken in, and a fortnight was given on a pavement and refused on the ground that a name on a piece of paper is worse than nothing, and nobody was asked and nobody asked anybody. From 0192, which of two opposite shapes this city has to stop, and what a person has to be holding to stop each — the second of them is still nothing. The volume's own, about a permission, an obligation and a right of return — untouched, and the word *permission* is at zero in all ten chapters and nobody in these ten raised the argument of the eleventh of April. And the sixth, about a document and not a person — no piece of paper was created in this batch; the things that came were a piece of scaffold, a folded metal frame, an upright cleaner, a piece of wire, a bag of plaster, a hand's worth of gravel and a bucket of washing-up water that was offered and refused, and a word in a row, and nobody has a copy of any of it.

- **AND FOUR FLAGS GO TO A HUMAN AND NONE OF THEM IS A WRITER'S. THE NAME OF THE CITY, WHICH THE PROSE CALLS TIDEGLASS, AND *NACRE* AND *RIVER STACKS* APPEAR NOWHERE IN CHAPTERS 315–324. WHAT VOLUME 05 IS CENTRALLY, WITH THE OPERATIVE RULES BINDING AND NO ROOT TERM EXPIRED AND NO RECLAMATION SEASON OPENED AND THE WORD *RECLAMATION* IN NO CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH. THE BOUNDARY, WHICH IS A HUMAN'S: `outline/series.md` GIVES VOLUME 04 AS 145–192, VOLUME 05 AS 193–240, VOLUME 06 AS 241–288 AND VOLUME 07 AS 289–336, THE PAGE HAS 195 THROUGH 324 IN THE VOLUME 05 DIRECTORY, THE TWO DISAGREE AT BOTH ENDS, THERE IS NO FIGURE FOR THE END OF VOLUME 05 IN EITHER, THE FIGURE 242 IS USED BY NOBODY, NO CHAPTER IS MOVED BETWEEN DIRECTORIES, NO OUTLINE IS REWRITTEN, NO VOLUME 06 OUTLINE AND NO VOLUME-CLOSE PROMPT EXISTS, AND CARD 0324 IS NOT A CLOSE. AND THE SPELLING OF THE THIRD OF THE EIGHT PLACES, WHICH WAS NOT TOUCHED IN THIS BATCH BECAUSE THE EIGHT PLACES ARE NOT READ OUT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, SO NEITHER SPELLING APPEARS AND THE FLAG CARRIES FORWARD UNDECIDED; **THE CENSUS OF THE STATE FILES STANDS AT FIVE FILES PRINTING BOTH SPELLINGS, ONE PRINTING ONLY ONE AND ONE PRINTING ONLY THE OTHER, AND THAT CENSUS IS CARRIED AT §11 OF THE `state/open-threads.md` BLOCK AND IS NOT REPEATED HERE.****

- **AND TWO FLAGS WERE TAKEN IN CONTINUATION 0013 AND BOTH RULES WERE KEPT. **CARD 0324 ASKED FOR *A BOROUGH, IN A MONTH*, WHICH ITS OWN COUNT DID NOT GIVE IT, AND THE RULE THAT BINDS WAS APPLIED ANYWAY: THE WORD *SPRING* IS AT ZERO IN ALL TEN CHAPTERS, NO CHAPTER CALLS ITS OWN MONTH A TURN OR A CHANGE OF FORTNIGHT OR A CHANGE OF ANYTHING, AND 0324 WAS GIVEN ORDINARY WEATHER INSTEAD, WHICH IS A DRY MORNING AND BIRDS IN A GUTTER.** AND THE PROMPT'S OWN BOUNDARY PAIR CAME OUT ONE DAY OUTSIDE ITS OWN ARITHMETIC AT BOTH ENDS, BEING TEN GAPS OF WHICH THE INCOMING ONE IS FORCED TO ONE DAY GIVEN AS THE SPAN OF THOSE TEN GAPS PLUS ONE DAY; COUNTED FROM 0314'S OWN DATE THE TWO BOUNDS ARE MONDAY 15 MAY AND TUESDAY 20 JUNE 2017 AND THE TENTH CHAPTER WENT TO SATURDAY THE TWENTY-SEVENTH OF MAY, WHICH SATISFIES ALL FOUR FIGURES. NOTHING WAS COMPRESSED AND NO CARD WAS DEFENDED. **THE NEXT PHASE IS A CONTINUATION BATCH, CHAPTERS 325 ONWARD, DATED FROM SUNDAY THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF MAY 2017, WHICH IS 0324's OWN DATE PLUS ONE, AND IT IS NOT A VOLUME CLOSE.** **AND A CHECKPOINT PASS ON 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 RE-MEASURED EVERY FIGURE ABOVE FROM THE CHAPTER FILES, FOUND AND REPAIRED SEVENTEEN DEFECTS IN TEN CHAPTERS AT THE SOURCE, AND FOUND THAT THIS FILE'S OWN *CURRENT CREATIVE PHASE* LINE HAD BEEN CARRYING CONTINUATION 0012'S DATES AND 0012'S WORD COUNTS UNDER 0013'S NAME, WHICH IS THE ERROR A LINE OF THIS KIND EXISTS TO CATCH AND WHICH IS NOW REPAIRED. THE NEXT PHASE'S PROMPT ITSELF MUST BE READ BACK FROM ITS FIRST WORD AFTER ANY EDIT TO IT.** **AND A REVIEW OF THIS BATCH ON 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 FOUND EIGHT CONCRETE THINGS, AND ALL EIGHT WERE REPAIRED AT THE SOURCE, AND THE BATCH WAS NOT RESTARTED AND NO CARD, DATE, DAY-COUNT, STANDING OR FLAG WAS CHANGED: 0324 SAID NINE IN A HALL WHOSE OWN ROSTER NAMES EIGHT AND READS EIGHT IN BOTH PLACES; 0323 INVERTED 0319's OWN TAP RULE AND HAD ITS JUGS FILL WITH NOBODY TURNING THE TAP, AND BOTH ARE REPAIRED; 0322 GAVE 0319's STRAIGHT NINE-TREAD FLIGHT A TURN, AND THE FLIGHT IS NOW STRAIGHT IN BREADTH; 0321 WAS 0311 WITH THE NOUNS SWAPPED AGAINST ITS OWN CARD, AND ITS SECOND HALF IS NOW THE WOMAN UNDER THE TWO SLATES ASKING FOR A BIT OF PAPER; 0315's CLOSING BLOCK PRINTED A CLOCK TIME ITS BODY NEVER GAVE; 0316 NAMED A MONTH AFTER THE TWENTY-THIRD OF MAY; AND THE SIX RUN FIGURES PRINTED FOR THIS BATCH COULD NOT BE REPRODUCIBLE BY ANY LATER WRITER BECAUSE NO TOKENISING RULE WAS WRITTEN DOWN BESIDE THEM. THE RUN FOUR REUSE COUNT WAS SIXTY BEFORE THAT PASS AND NIL AFTER IT, AND THE SIXTY WERE THE HAND-OVER BLOCKS SAYING THE CHAPTERS IN THE CHAPTERS' OWN WORDS. THE METHOD, THE NINE-TOKEN THRESHOLD, THE STRUCTURAL DATELINE DETECTION, THE CONTIGUITY CHECK AND A TWO-PAIR POSITIVE CONTROL ARE NOW IN THE 0014 PROMPT, AND THE SIX CORRECTED FIGURES ARE 7 RAW AND NIL, 16 RAW AND NIL, 18/11/5 RAW AND NIL, 31 RAW AND NIL, 31 RAW AND NIL, AND NIL.**

## THE STATE OF THE CITY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 334 — Saturday the eighth of July 2017, from about ten o'clock at night, in this city

**NINE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIVE DAYS HAVE GONE UNDER THE NINETEENTH ENTRY OF A REGISTER IN A BUILDING BY THE WATER AND THE LINE IS STILL BLANK, A GLAZED LANTERN ON A FLAT ROOF OVER A FIRST-FLOOR CORRIDOR LET WATER IN AND A WOMAN PUT A BOWL UNDER IT HERSELF, THREE CLOSE-BOARD GATES WERE LEFT IN THREE YARDS ON ONE LANE IN TWO MONTHS AND A MAN SAID THE NUMBER THREE OUT LOUD FOR THE FIRST TIME IN HIS LIFE, A MAN SAID AT A DOOR THAT HE WOULD HAVE A FENCE CLOSED AND DID NOT AND DID NOT DENY SAYING IT, A LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN WENT THROUGH ONE DAY IN FIVE ROOMS WITH FOUR OF THE ROOMS FULL OF PEOPLE AND SAID SOMETHING IN FRONT OF THREE OF THEM, AND NOBODY HAS ANSWERED ANYTHING.**

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN AND THE LINE UNDER IT IS NINE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIVE DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK.** It stands on the second shelf up in a bay off a first-floor corridor in the building by the water, on a door that does not shut over the catch. The last of the nineteen is a yard lamp, entered as gone in about November two years ago under a heading reserved for that sort of thing, and nothing has been written under that reserved heading since. Below that line the sheet goes on a couple of fingers and then stops about two inches short of the foot of the book, and nobody left that gap; the book has it. **THE ONLY PERSON WHO HAS SAID THAT FIGURE ALOUD IS THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE, WHO SAYS SHE COUNTED IT HERSELF OFF A CALENDAR IN HER KITCHEN AND COUNTED IT TWICE, AND NOBODY IN THE ROOM SHE SAID IT IN questioned her about the length of her own count, and THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES SAID NOBODY'S GOING TO CHECK IT AND SHE SAID SHE WAS NOT ASKING ANYBODY TO.**
- **AND THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING.** the warmest day of that year to date, with the tar giving underfoot by early afternoon · a man on a ladder at a shutter at the far end of that parade at a quarter to nine on his own, folding the ladder and carrying it twenty yard to the next one, with nobody helping him and him asking nobody · a van at the fish shop at ten past nine with no lettering on either side, standing about ten minutes and not coming back · **a man going up the low end of a street at about half past nine with a bag over his shoulder and coming back down it at about a quarter to ten without it, and nine indoors, and not one of the nine going to the window to see** · and a shower off the roofs at about ten that goes through without reaching that hall and takes the tar from soft to hard in about four minutes.
- **A WHITE VAN, TWICE, A MONTH APART, AND NOBODY ASKING ANYBODY.** On Sunday the twenty-eighth of May it stood across the gate of a yard behind the sixth of nine shops on that parade, and the man in his thirties could not get his own van out, waited about an hour and a half, moved his own out by inches with a bucket under a wheel, and did the remainder of that Sunday on his feet, walking a mile and a quarter to a job he could have driven to. **On Thursday the twenty-ninth of June it stood across the back gate of the shop where the woman who works in a shop is standing, having entered forwards and reversed in there, and a man with a trolley could not get his front wheels through, and she would not have six boxes put down on a pavement with about nine inches of it, so he carried them in on a sheet of his own in two goes while she pointed at the two heavy ones.** Five working hours went, on a day whose pay depends on the job being finished and nobody in that parade knows a van was ever in that passage. **Neither of them was ever told about the other, neither asked a question, and she has said she is not going to knock on anybody's door and not going to take up position in that alley watching every vehicle that enters, because the first one she stopped she would have to stop the next.**
- **A SENTENCE ABOUT A FIRM, AND A QUESTION NOBODY ANSWERED.** In the hall on Carrow Lane, on a meter, on the third of June, the man in his sixties put down that a man at the works told him a firm down the estuary that does the outsides of houses and roofs has gone over and that you cannot get anybody — and that he has not got the name of it, and that the name of it had been painted on a vehicle that has since gone. The man in his forties would not let it be counted: a thing heard twice is not a thing checked, is not a smaller version of a thing checked, and is not a second of the same. **The woman who works in a shop carried it into the room at the back of her shop in her own words and the man who works nights put a question about its identity to her and was given nothing.** **AND THE SAME MORNING A BAR CAME OFF A BACK DOOR AND CANNOT GO BACK ON.** A man arrived with an under-counter fridge on a hand truck and it would not go through a back door with a steel bar on the outside of it. The man who works nights said take the bar off; she would not, because two houses behind that yard had their doors off their hinges twice in a fortnight in March; the man with the hand truck would not stand in a doorway with a back door open on an alley. The bar came off, the fridge went in, and the fridge is then inside the swing of a door that will not close against it, so the bar cannot be hooked back on and its key is in an office in a town that shut in the autumn. **It stays off, and nobody will be able to say why, because saying it is a fridge means saying what is behind the fridge.**
- **TWO JOBS ON ONE LANDING, AND A MAN WHO CAME OUT TWICE IN HIS SOCKS.** A new sash in a first-floor landing will not go up — not rotten, painted shut about four times, the top rail swollen an eighth of an inch into the outer stop — and it is a two-man job because somebody has to hold the head of it while somebody works the bottom rail. **That building's ground floor tenant has held the head of a sash for him three times and is the only man he can think of who would, and he came out at about ten on his own, said before anything was asked that it is a two-man job and that he is on at four and the trestle is across his own front door, and went back in.** The second job is the same morning: a woman in the middle flat took her own front door off its hinges on the Sunday to get a wardrobe up and cannot get it back, because the top left screw has gone into air where that corner of the frame has dropped half an inch. **He can pack the corner alone and cannot steady a door against a closing frame while drawing the same hinge tight.** Both stand. **The only person told any of it asked how often that landing has come to this, and the answer is that nobody has ever counted anything in this town and no one has ever been given a pencil on a landing and asked to write, and that it could be dozens or it could be four and there is no way of telling which.**
